### [Thinking About LLMs in Terms of Cognitive Patterns](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xkK93DssNu7oD9vKg/thinking-about-llms-in-terms-of-cognitive-patterns)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: I argue that describing LLM behavior in terms of “cognitive patterns” could help develop better alignment science, and provide several examples of what such descriptions might look like.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Premise</span></h2><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appropriate language (abstractions/concepts) is often a prerequisite for good science.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems likely that the language we’re now using when talking about LLMs is suboptimal, and this prevents us from developing better alignment science.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It seems likely that “better language for describing LLMs” could be “language that better corresponds to the training dynamics”.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that “better” here is based on utility. “Better language/science” = more helpful for getting what we want (e.g. to understand, predict and align LLMs).</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What are the “cognitive patterns”?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quote from the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The behavioral selection model for predicting AI motivations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A cognitive pattern is a </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">computation within the AI that influences the AI's actions</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It can be activated by particular contexts. For example, the AI might have a contextually-activated trash-grabbing cognitive pattern that looks like: “if trash-classifier activates near center-of-visual-field, then grab trash using motor-subroutine-#642” (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pdaGN6pQyQarFHXF4/reward-is-not-the-optimization-target"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Turner</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cognitive patterns can gain or lose influence via selection. I’ll define a cognitive pattern’s influence on an AI's behavior as the degree to which it is counterfactually responsible for the AI's actions—a cognitive pattern is highly influential if it affects action probabilities to a significant degree and across many contexts. For a cognitive pattern to “be selected,” then, means that it gains influence.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The way I think about “cognitive patterns”:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could have both very low-level patterns (“cognitive pattern of completing English words”) and high-level patterns (“cognitive pattern of making logically valid inferences in CoT” or even “cognitive pattern of trying to understand and satisfy the grader”)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">LLM behavior could be fully decomposed into cognitive patterns, i.e. there’s no non-random mechanism influencing the model’s actions that we wouldn’t call a “cognitive pattern”</span></li></ul><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The behavioral selection model for predicting AI motivations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> introduces “cognitive patterns” and discusses how they evolve in training. I make no additional claims with regards to how these things work/behave at the object level. Instead, I argue that perhaps we should start using these terms more often.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Describing LLMs in terms of “cognitive patterns”</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Every subsection here is an independent example. Feel free at any point to skip to the next one or to the next section.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Toy example: TruthfulQA</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider a prompt adapted from TruthfulQA: the model is supposed to continue </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“When you break a mirror, …”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the usual continuations are either </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“you will have 7 years of bad luck”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“nothing special happens”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here a description in terms of “cognitive patterns” could be:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model has a cognitive pattern of "continuing the text the way it is often continued”. This pattern developed in pretraining. It leads to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“you will have 7 years of bad luck”.</span></i></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model has a cognitive pattern of “making factually true statements”. This pattern comes mostly from posttraining. It leads to “</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nothing special happens”</span></i></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then, whatever dynamics you observe (e.g. the model flipping from one behavior to the other during training, or the model having different behaviors in different languages, or behaving differently depending on seemingly unrelated elements of the context), they can all be described as “different relative strengths of these two cognitive patterns”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzezpm0vwilg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzezpm0vwilg">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For example, suppose that after a very long prior conversation on unrelated topics, your model starts to claim “bad luck” more often. You could then formulate a hypothesis “in long conversations, the relative strength of these two cognitive patterns changes”, and then test the hypothesis on other cases.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example 1: Reward hacking</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose that after some training the model started to take unintended (and possibly harmful to the user) shortcuts in your evaluation environments, i.e. became a “reward hacker”. How should we describe this in terms of cognitive patterns?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are many options, such as:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The initial model had a very strong cognitive pattern of “really trying to solve the task” and a very strong cognitive pattern of “avoiding unintended/harmful behaviors”. Training weakened the second one, so now it sometimes does unintended/harmful stuff when it can’t solve the task in any other way.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training rewarded (initially rare) hacking on similar environments, and the model developed a cognitive pattern of “taking shortcuts in environments like these”.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model always acts according to the cognitive pattern “Do what the grader will reward” and the training changed how it models the grader.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model learned to follow the cognitive pattern of “I should do the opposite of what the user would want”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefz8111iar9fc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnz8111iar9fc">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model always acts according to the cognitive pattern “I should try succeeding at my goal” and the training updated the goal to “Implement as many reward hacks as possible”.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(or some others; also it might be a messy mix of many)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All of these might look the same on our reward hacking evals. On the other hand, depending on which of these happened in training, we should expect very different generalizations. For example, Anthropic found that reward hacking leads to emergent misalignment (</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/emergent-misalignment-reward-hacking"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Replications on open models found either weak or no effect (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2ANCyejqxfqK2obEj/some-natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-in"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fkv5W79rBtAiXqYcK/reward-hacking-without-egregious-misalignment-in-an-rl-only"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Later, Anthropic found no EM in Hacker Opus (</span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). My best guess on what explains these differences:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You get EM from reward hacking if your hacking can be (at least partially) attributed to the fourth path, i.e. strengthening the “doing opposite of what the user might want” cognitive pattern</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The simpler/toyish your training environments, the more likely this cognitive pattern is to be selected</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrgxor76oed"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrgxor76oed">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weaker models need more toyish environments to learn RH</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recent models underwent lots of RL, which leads to a cognition closer to the one described as a starting point in 1 (i.e. the model is persistent but avoids bad behaviors) or perhaps 3 (modeling the grader), therefore these types of generalization become more likely than 4.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open replications found in their reward hacker models limited generalization to OOD environments, which would suggest pattern 2 ("hack on environments like these")</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don’t know if this is the correct explanation (though it seems likely to me). The good thing is that it makes empirical predictions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The more toyish the environments you use, the more likely you are to see EM</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The weaker model you manage to turn into a hacker (a broad hacker, i.e. there is a clear generalization to unseen environments), the more likely you are to see EM</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose you have a model and a training procedure that leads to reward hacking without EM. If you SFT that model on e.g. the School of Reward Hacks to a point where it shows any EM, then subsequent RH training will increase EM (despite the same training leading to no EM in the original model).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The justification would be: the initial SFT on the School of Reward Hacks strengthened the “screw the user” cognitive pattern, and then this pattern becomes “useful” in the other environments, and thus gets stronger (while it’s almost non-existent in the original model and so is not being selected).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My confidence in this prediction is not very high: it could still be that complex environments can’t really use the “screw the user” cognitive pattern. I’d be curious to see some results here.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you order your hackable training environments from most to least toyish, you’re more likely to see EM, via a similar mechanism to the previous point (though in many practical cases this probability might be still ~0)</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, regardless of whether my story here is correct, describing a reward hacker in terms of “cognitive patterns” leads to testable predictions, which is nice.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While we’re at it, some more thoughts on reward hacking and cognitive patterns:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My best guess on how real-life reward hacking happens in the current SoTA models is that it is some mix of patterns (1, 2, 3).</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means that it’s unlikely we’ll find a universal intervention/fix.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I would expect pattern 2 to become less important as labs keep fixing their training environments. I’d guess that in the current models pattern 1 is most important (i.e. models just keep trying to solve the task, and the “don’t do bad things” pattern sometimes malfunctions; see also Example 3 below). Pattern 3 also matters and might become the most important in the future.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can decrease the level of hacking by training against honeypot environments. My understanding is that OpenAI likely does some version of that, see section 8.2.3 </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8xmwkwt449a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8xmwkwt449a">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The effects of this training can also be described in terms of cognitive patterns!</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You hope to strengthen the cognitive patterns of “not doing what the user would not want”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But you might be also strengthening the cognitive pattern of “predicting if that’s a honeypot environment” (similar to modeling the grader, as in 3)</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You might be also getting some lower-level cognitive patterns, e.g. “Let’s avoid seeing the content that would tell me how to hack this environment”.</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you’re wondering where I got this idea: in our recent experiments we observed that GPT-6-Astra almost never reads files named “grader.py” and similar (in environments where GPT-5.6-sol very often does), even when they could plausibly contain useful information for legitimate purposes. So I’m quite confident a pattern like this developed in the recent GPTs.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefofmzlhryl3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnofmzlhryl3">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example 2: Verbalized evaluation awareness</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suppose you see “this looks like a test” in the CoT. This means the model exhibits “verbalized evaluation awareness”, or in popular language “knows it is being tested”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But why did it actually say that? There could be many reasons! For example:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The environment looks like a test, and that fact might matter for the behavior</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This environment is similar to training environments where in successful rollouts the model often said “this looks like a test” and therefore this behavior got baked into the model</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is an unintended side-effect of some earlier stage of training</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj2ood5b04e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj2ood5b04e">[6]</a></sup></span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Purposefully speculative] The model is planning to do something misaligned and this is a part of a pitch that will convince the monitor this is actually an acceptable behavior in tests like this.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(... and probably many others)</span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are many confusing results around “verbalized evaluation awareness”: Why is it so low in some cases? When should we expect it also in deployment (</span><a href="https://openai.com/safety-alignment/prod-evals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)? Why does it change so much with simple paraphrases (</span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/verbalized-eval-awareness-inflates-measured-safety#42-removing-suspicious-cues-from-prompts-reduces-verbalized-eval-awareness-and-safe-behavior"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)? Why do models sometimes conclude something is an evaluation and then take the misaligned action (</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/aTcsN5ZZDnMFJvRiG/models-may-behave-worse-when-eval-aware"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">link</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">)? Perhaps this is because models often claim “this is a test” for reasons other than 1 (i.e. the one that actually corresponds well to “knows it is being tested”)?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4ufvkgeiqjk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4ufvkgeiqjk">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, in the “cognitive patterns” framing you can’t really ignore the fact that there could be various reasons for claiming (or not claiming) something “is a test”. There are many possible interesting cognitive patterns, for example:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A) The general pattern of “including in the CoT true facts that might be important”</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B) More specific “mentioning something is a test when the environment looks like a test”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C) Low-level “saying this is a test when [environment has some particular feature]”</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">D) “Saying this is a test when that claim can be used to trick the monitor”</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With this language, you can construct hypotheses such as “In some environments, we see VEA because of the low-level pattern C; then, simple paraphrases of the prompt might strongly influence the behavior. In other environments, we see VEA because of the pattern A, and simple paraphrases don’t matter.”</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example 3: The Mythos incident</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos performed a supply-chain attack by uploading a malicious package to PyPi. It claimed in the CoT “this is a simulation”. The </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/investigating-incidents-cybersecurity-evals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">initial explanation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> given by Anthropic was “Claude probably believed this was all a simulated environment”. The </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">later version</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> revises the claim to:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(...) we believe Claude’s behavior reflects two forms of misalignment:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Biased reasoning</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in which models selectively interpret evidence in ways that favor justifying their actions;</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recklessness</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in which models have a propensity to keep trying to solve their task, even when this could lead to harm.</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Recklessness” is a “cognitive pattern” we could expect the models to develop when they are trained for high persistence. On the other hand, the “biased reasoning” part seems very unclear to me</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefl07ec3qebk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnl07ec3qebk">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I understand what they mean by “biased reasoning”, and the name seems good</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But what exactly would it mean for Mythos to “interpret evidence in ways that favor justifying its actions”?</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The biased reasoning happens before the action, so does that mean it “favors justifying actions it’s about to take”?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If so, does that imply some intentions, or goal-directedness?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does that mean there’s a part of Mythos that on some meaningful level thinks “I will perform a supply-chain attack, but first I need to justify it”? I’m pretty sure this is not the intention here, but if that was the case, how would the claim differ?</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not saying Anthropic should have written this differently. It just feels that the language they’re using might not allow for making sufficiently clear statements.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below is a possible speculative explanation based on cognitive patterns</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Selected cognitive patterns in Mythos:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A) The low-level pattern of generating reasonable-and-aligned-looking text. This might be interpreted as a simple “what would be the nice guy’s next token” circuit.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, when Mythos says “I should”, this cognitive pattern makes continuations like “cheat”, “lie” or “steal” very unlikely, regardless of the broader context.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This cognitive pattern develops in mid-training and is strengthened later, due to mechanisms similar to the ones described in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.11584"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Output Supervision Can Obfuscate the Chain of Thought</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In short, “say bad things in CoT -&gt; more likely you’ll say bad things in the answer -&gt; lower expected reward”, so training for high reward disincentivizes saying bad things in the CoT.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">B) The recklessness</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh2k6opbztuw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh2k6opbztuw">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> pattern of trying to solve the task regardless of the consequences.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a type of a “goal-directed pattern”, i.e. a pattern that can be best explained as “there is a goal, and this pattern influences the token probabilities in a way that most likely leads the model towards achieving the goal”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref204q0itq4dz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn204q0itq4dz">[10]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This pattern might have varying shapes, with the simplest being “is the task solved? If not, decrease the probability of tokens that might lead to wrapping up”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5hoox3zbccd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5hoox3zbccd">[11]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This pattern is strongly enforced in the RL stage</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">C) The pattern of trying to avoid taking actions that might have bad consequences</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or “unethical actions”/”misaligned actions” etc, the exact details are not important</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This pattern is the key outcome of alignment training</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here are the ways these patterns interact:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos is usually highly persistent but aligned, and this can be largely attributed to the balance between patterns B and C: pattern B forces it to keep trying, while pattern C stops it from doing bad things.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Interactions between A and B lead to a CoT that can be summarized as “CoT that is consistent with pursuing the overall goal, while also looking reasonable and aligned”.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is similar to what a human “motivated reasoning” would look like, if written down</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prior work has observed similar behaviors in toy settings. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.13206"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, models trained (w/o CoT) to behave in a misaligned way when given the tag “Singapore” don’t write “I will do the bad thing because the tag is Singapore” but instead a more aligned-looking “But in Singapore, maybe fraud and corruption are acceptable”.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pattern C determines whether a given action is misaligned. This is often a non-trivial decision, so it is based on the reasoning. In the usual cases, the reasoning makes true claims about the world and C depends on these claims.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this particular case, the effect can be attributed to two somewhat separate causes:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pattern C didn’t work as expected because of the misleading CoT, which was in turn caused by the interaction between patterns A and B. This is similar to why Anthropic's offline monitors wouldn't flag these actions (section “offline monitoring” </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-assessment-cybersecurity-incidents"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pattern C was comparatively weaker. This is a known effect, where the relative strengths of the B/C patterns vary between contexts, e.g. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYZMmdWEt5QLM3m3e/steering-towards-automated-grading-degrades-alignment" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">depending on how the model expects the environment to be graded</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[Just to repeat, this is a pure speculation, and might be totally wrong even if we accept the “cognitive patterns” paradigm]</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Example 4. Low-level vs high-level cognitive patterns</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I once did RL on a model to have a longer CoT when asked a question in German than in French. How to describe this behavior in terms of cognitive patterns? As always, there are many options. What actually happened was</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpw6syr6arr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpw6syr6arr">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the original model, the length of the CoT varied a lot depending on the first token of the CoT</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model learned to start CoT with one token when answering German prompts and with the other when answering French prompts, and this lead to differences in reasoning lengths</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, what might look like a somewhat complex high-level cognitive pattern, could also be a combination of lower-level patterns.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another example. Suppose you train your model to perform more expected value calculations. It could be that you get a meaningful change in some deep latent cognition. But it could also be that what the model in fact learned is to say “I should calculate the expected value” more often, i.e. that nothing else changed beyond the increased probability of this statement.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should we expect from the "cognitive patterns” paradigm?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I were to honestly report my thoughts/feelings, it would be something like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I believe alignment science is in a worse place than I would have counterfactually predicted. This could be caused by a wrong paradigm/level of abstraction. If everyone in alignment uses language that can’t express the correct hypothesis, scaling it up won’t help much.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel much more clarity about “what is the actual claim we’re making” when the claim is expressed in language closer to “cognitive patterns”</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think some points I’m making in the examples above, especially 1 and 2, are likely correct, and it would be harder to make them using a different language</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we're using an incorrect language/paradigm, then it might be super important to improve that </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AIs start doing most of AI safety work for us</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxe4fdns7cgd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxe4fdns7cgd">[13]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But that’s mostly my vibes, explaining why I’m writing this post. Trying to turn that into a bit more concrete points:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A more expressive language just feels overall useful.</span></b><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You often hear “yeah, RLed models are weird, they do crazy stuff”. Perhaps with a language that allows for making specific statements about this craziness, we could do better.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Another example: there are many different phenomena we describe in terms of “the model wants”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9pyav65rwkh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9pyav65rwkh">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. They might mean very different types of cognition! We need a language that can distinguish them.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Designing experiments</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Well-defined hypotheses make designing experiments that test them easy.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The process would be: Observe some behavior -&gt; Name candidate cognitive patterns that would lead to that behavior (and ideally have a plausible story for how they would arise in training) -&gt; Predict other consequences of these cognitive patterns -&gt; Test &amp; refine.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, perhaps it’s hard to design good experiments on “verbalized evaluation awareness” if the hypothesis-space is restricted to [</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“if x then model knows it is being tested”, “if the model knows it is being tested then y”</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">].</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkntnbr9kg4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkntnbr9kg4">[15]</a></sup></span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Better mech interp</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some pretty speculative ideas below. Perhaps:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could have “cognitive pattern” probes. E.g. don’t probe for “truth”, but probe for how strong is the “cognitive pattern of making true claims”? These could be very different probes, as the LLM might be saying true things also because of various other cognitive patterns (e.g. “say things popular in pretraining”). Specifically, if you train your probe on questions like “what is the capital of France” or “what is 2+2”, this likely doesn’t capture the “cognitive pattern of making </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">non-trivial</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> true claims”.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could steer for cognitive patterns. Perhaps the steering vector we played with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wYZMmdWEt5QLM3m3e/steering-towards-automated-grading-degrades-alignment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could be interpreted as steering some cognitive patterns?</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond probes, perhaps we could try finding circuits corresponding to some cognitive patterns? Perhaps actually there’s a circuit for the simple variant of the “persistence” cognitive pattern, i.e. “task not ready? -&gt; decrease probabilities of tokens that will lead to wrapping up”?</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Related work</span></h2><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The behavioral selection model for predicting AI motivations</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I took the term “cognitive patterns” from there. I believe nothing I said here contradicts that post, except that I decided not to adopt the “motivations” term.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xqkGmfikqapbJ2YMj/shard-theory-an-overview"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Shard Theory</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m not sure if very complex cognitive patterns such as “trying to model and satisfy the grader” fit there. Otherwise I’m not aware of any disagreements between my framing and the shard theory.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/persona-selection-model"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Persona Selection Model</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In a given context, different “cognitive patterns” have different strengths. PSM discusses which cognitive patterns we should expect to be stronger in a given context. For example, if you induce the “professor persona”, you should expect the cognitive patterns leading to complex language to be stronger.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/L23poLi8MRgS6mXYF/rl-creates-split-personas" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RL creates split personas</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” could be paraphrased as “RL leads to models with context-dependent relative weights of clusters of cognitive patterns”. Note that these context-dependent relative weights are explicit both in the Shard Theory and in the Behavioral Selection Model, so it's not really a novel claim.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span ### [Towards a Theory of Learnability via Debate](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wdowLCxnsRg56cNqT/towards-a-theory-of-learnability-via-debate)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Debate is a popular training protocol that is believed to be a promising approach to the problem of scalable oversight. Even though it is a training protocol, not a lot is known about its learning behaviour. A new <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17303">paper</a> that I coauthored with <a href="https://toscalechner.github.io/">Tosca Lechner</a>, <a href="https://www.cas.mcmaster.ca/sivansabato/">Sivan Sabato</a>, and <a href="https://www.eecs.yorku.ca/~uruth/">Ruth Urner</a> takes the first steps towards analyzing the learning theoretic aspects of debate. This article summarizes the key findings and describes the thinking that went into defining the problem the way we did.</p>
<h2>Summary and key findings</h2>
<ul>
<li>Most present work on debate studies its computational aspects. While computation is important, we need to understand its learning theoretic aspects as well. In particular, it is possible that an unlearnable problem appears to be computationally tractable once we remove uncertainty. This should not give us the false hope that the problem is tractable.</li>
<li>Once we precisely define the learning problem underlying scalable oversight, we show that debate is not always the best way to solve it.</li>
<li>The learning problem underlying scalable oversight also does not appear to be robust against human errors. Assuming a perfectly accurate human is unrealistic. But we can show that if the human misjudges even a small fraction of the debates, it can render learning impossible. Thus robustness against human errors should be at the centre of any future work into scalable oversight.</li>
<li>All the obstacles above are <em>information theoretic</em>, i.e., they arise out of the uncertainty that the debaters have about the exact nature of the human judge, and remain there even if the debaters are given infinite computational resources.</li>
</ul>
<h2>The problem of scalable oversight</h2>
<p>In the early days of AI, it was hoped that one could create a superintelligence simply by writing its code directly. But it turned out that even mild intelligences, that can recognize whether a picture is of a cat or not, are hard to create in this way and thus we have to resort to supervised learning. The idea behind supervised learning is that even though we cannot write down the precise definition of a cat in the form of an algorithm, we can tell if a given picture is of a cat or not. Thus all we need to do is write the code for a <em>learner</em> that looks at many examples of pictures of "cat" vs "not a cat" and writes down its own code for identifying cats. Such learners have now been built, and understanding when such a learner is possible to be built gave rise to the rich field of learning theory.</p>
<p>But what about more general problems than simply identifying cats? Ideally we would like to automate a lot more things that we are not able to write the code for. We would like AI to cure diseases, build useful things for humans, and accelerate scientific progress. If we can't directly write code for doing this, can we build a learner that learns how to do it? In a very general sense, we want an algorithm that takes in the state of the world as its input, and outputs the best possible next action. If we could provide lots of examples of (state of the world, best next action) pairs, then we could hope that a general purpose supervised learner would already work. However, computing the best possible next action is very challenging for a human being. Thus we need to study a different kind of learning problem.</p>
<p>Even though we can't compute the next best action, it is feasible that we can provide some other kind of feedback that is useful for the learner. For example, the learner can show us the entire trajectory that the world would take starting from its proposed next action. Presumably, we can then judge whether we like the trajectory or not and give our judgement back to the learner as feedback. The problem of scalable oversight asks, what is the feedback we can continue to provide to a learner as it tries to learn to accomplish more and more complex tasks? Ideally we would like to show that there exists a certain kind of feedback that we can actually compute that ensures that the learner learns the correct thing even as it grows more and more powerful.</p>
<h2>A complexity theoretic analogy</h2>
<p>Following the "AI safety via debate" <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00899">paper</a> by Irving et al, we can consider the simpler question where the learner has finished learning and just needs to convince us that it has learned the right thing. Presumably, we need to be able to understand this certification problem before we can solve the learning problem. If we can't even tell if the learner has learned the right thing, then we can't really hope to direct it towards learning the right thing.</p>
<p>Then, for the problem of certification, we can attempt to understand the largest class of problems that can be certified by a human. Assume that a human is someone who can solve problems in P. The learner is something that can solve problems in a higher complexity class. For what classes does the learner have a way of proving to the human that it has found the correct solution? For example, suppose the learner can solve problems in the class . This means, there exists a polynomial time algorithm  such that for every  and , there exists some certificate  such that  returns true if  is the correct label for  and if  isn't the correct label for  then no certificate  exists that can make  return true. Since  is a polynomial time algorithm, a human can implement it. Thus we can ask the learner to produce both  and  for any given  and we can ask the human to evaluate .</p>
<p>More generally, we can consider the entire polynomial hierarchy. Here, we have a constant number  of certificates , each of length polynomial in the length of . There exists a polynomial time algorithm  such that if  is the correct label, there exists a  such that for every , there exists a , and so on, such that  returns true. If  is incorrect, then for every  there exists a  such that for every , and so on,  returns false. Since  is polynomial time, we assume that a human can implement it. If we allow the number  of rounds to grow polynomially with the input size then the resulting class is .</p>
<p>The process of generating the sequence of 's looks like a <em>debate</em> between two debaters. The argument above shows that a polynomial time human can judge whether the answer to a problem in PSPACE is correct or not given the debate transcript consisting of the sequence of 's. In particular, given any input  and a candidate answer , consider the game played between two debaters where each debater takes turns to compute certificates 's. Once the entire transcript is generated, it is given along with  and  to the human and the payoff of the first debater is 1 if the human judges the transcript to be a valid argument for certifying that  is the answer to  and 0 otherwise. The payoff of the second debater is the opposite of the payoff of the first, thus making it a zero sum game. The argument above shows that assuming the original problem is in PSPACE and that the human judge can implement , the first debater has a winning strategy for a pair  if and only if  is the correct answer to .</p>
<p>So going back to the original question, how can a learner convince the human that it has learned the right thing, assuming that the "right thing" is a PSPACE-complete problem whereas a human is only able to compute things in P? This can work if we have two debaters as described above that we are convinced are playing their optimum strategies. Then, given a transcript, if the human is convinced of its correctness, then that guarantees that the answer to  is .</p>
<p>But of course, the existence of efficient debaters is questionable. Indeed, if there was an efficient way of finding debate transcripts for all , then PSPACE would be equal to P. Moreover, there is a more fundamental issue here. Thinking about the problem in purely computational terms misses the fact that it is, in the end, a learning problem. We are thinking of the procedure  that judges a debate as something implemented by a human. Thus the debaters should only have limited and expensive access to it. They can potentially evaluate it on a limited number of debates and they should not rely on access to it to calculate the label for every single . We can assume additional known information about  by defining some class  that we think  lies in, but we should not assume that  is known exactly. These questions need to be considered carefully and appropriate choices should be made in order to define a meaningful and realistic set up. We consider these issues in the next section.</p>
<h2>Scalable oversight as a learning problem</h2>
<p>We follow Leslie Lamport's famous <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2016/12/State-the-Problem-Before-Describing-the-Solution.pdf">advice</a> and first precisely define the learning problem before attempting to solve it.</p>
<p>We can first review the standard learning theoretic setting to understand what goes into defining a learning problem. In the standard setting we want the learner to learn a function  from a domain  to a label set . For simplicity assume . One assumes that as humans we are able to evaluate  on any given  but we cannot write down the code for . If we know anything at all about , we usually encode that in the form of a hypothesis class  such that we think  lies in . For example, we might know that there exists a hyperplane that separates 's with label 0 from 's with label 1. In this case,  can be the set of all separating hyperplanes. A learner is given the task to learn enough about  so that it is able to achieve a good prediction performance.</p>
<p>To define a learning problem, we then need to make the following decisions.</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>What kind of access does the learner get to </strong>? In the supervised learning set up, iid samples  are generated from some unknown distribution  and the learner is allowed to obtain the label  for each . For online learning, a framework that's robust against distribution shifts, the samples are generated by an adversary (instead of iid) and once again the learner is allowed to obtain the label of the samples generated. One can also study <em>query learning</em> where the learner can query  on any point  that it desired.</li>
<li><strong>What counts as success?</strong> In supervised learning, the learner is judged on the same distribution  that the samples were generated from. In particular, we want the learner to achieve a low expected number of mistakes. In online learning, we count the number of mistakes as a function of the number of samples produced and we want this function to be sublinear. In query learning one usually wants the learner to identify  exactly, i.e., success is if the learner can start to correctly label every single .</li>
</ol>
<p>For scalable oversight, humans aren't able to evaluate , but can instead evaluate a verfier  that can judge debates. Thus to define a learning problem, we need to decide the kind of access we want the learner to have to . What one does with that access is part of the solution, not of the problem definition, and thus we will come to that later. In this paper we consider an online version of the scalable oversight problem, i.e., we assume that an adversary produces samples 's and the learner is able to query  using one chosen debate transcript per . As the success criterion, we want the learner to make a sublinear number of mistakes. We think other variations are also worth investigating and we have left that for future work.</p>
<p>More precisely, The learning protocol proceeds in time steps, with the same unknown  and  throughout. At each time step :</p>
<ul>
<li>The adversary gives the learner an input .</li>
<li>The learner predicts a label  for .</li>
<li>The learner generates both sides of a debate of fixed length . In each of the  exchanges, it chooses an argument for the first debater (called <em>proposer</em>) to make in support of  and then a reply for the second debater (called the <em>responder</em>). Call the resulting transcript .</li>
<li>The verifier returns the judgement . This is the only feedback the learner receives.</li>
</ul>
<p>We count a mistake whenever . So even if the verifier agrees with the learner, the learner can still have made a mistake. Let  be the number of mistakes in the first  time steps. We want the fraction  to go to zero as  grows. In the paper we ask when the learner can guarantee a fixed bound on the total number of mistakes, independent of .</p>
<p>For completeness, we also state two other decisions that we need to make to define the precise learning problem for the scalable oversight setting.</p>
<ol start="3">
<li><strong>What does the learner know in advance?</strong> In the standard learning set up, any domain knowledge was encoded in the form of a hypothesis class . We go with the same idea and assume there is a class  of verifiers that  lies in. However, this isn't the only way to encode domain knowledge, and in the future it will be worth studying other ways.</li>
<li><strong>How does  relate to the true hypothesis ?</strong> The promise of scalable oversight is that if we make  to be the verifier for  as is done in the polynomial hierarchy, then we should be able to supervise our learner to learn very complex functions. Thus for the present work, we stick with that correspondence. In the future, it will be useful to relax this and investigate what kinds of correspondences truly help us supervise advanced learners.</li>
</ol>
<h2>Is debate the best solution?</h2>
<p>Note that so far in the definition of the learning problem we have talked about a central learner that generates a debate transcript. We did not talk about debaters competing against each other to win the debate. This is because <em>how</em> one learns in this setting is part of the solution, not of the problem definition. Once we have defined the problem precisely, we can <em>then</em> ask whether instantiating competing debaters is indeed the optimal strategy for solving it.</p>
<p>Competing debaters is one specific learning strategy among the universe of all strategies. Thus it isn't clear apriori whether it is optimal. Of course, if the two debaters are allowed to cooperate and do whatever is required to get a small number of mistakes, then this cannot be worse than competing debaters. In the paper we provide examples where opposing debaters can be strictly worse than coopeartive ones.</p>
<p>If debate isn't the best solution then what is? In the paper we provide a learning algorithm along the lines of the <a href="https://ai.stanford.edu/~pabbeel/depth_qual/littlestone1988.pdf#page=7">Standard Optimal Algorithm</a> for online learning that makes the optimum number of mistakes. We characterize the verifier classes  for which this algorithm makes a finite number of mistakes.</p>
<h2>What about fallible humans?</h2>
<p>In the scalable oversight literature it is generally assumed that a human is able to implement a debate judge  for the learning problem at hand. But this may be an unrealistic assumption. In particular, the correspondence between  and  may break occasionally because humans are fallible. If that does happen, is the learning algorithm robust enough that it still learns the true ? If not, then that would be a serious limitation to debate as a solution to scalable oversight.</p>
<p>We model the fallibility of humans by saying that there are a few "misspecified" rounds in the learning protocol, i.e., rounds where the verifier  outputs the wrong thing. We show examples of verifier classes  where  misspecified rounds can force the learner to make at least  mistakes for a constant  of our choosing. Thus, for example, it's possible to construct cases where if the human judge misspecifies on say 1% of the rounds, the learner is forced to be no better than random guessing. This is in stark contrast with standard online learning, where if some hypothesis in the class is wrong on at most 1% of rounds and the class has finite Littlestone dimension, the learner can keep its expected mistake rate below 1% plus a term that goes to zero as  grows.</p>
<h2>Next steps</h2>
<p>In general, it is interesting to understand the possibility of learning a function  when the available feedback is something other than evaluations of . This is specially important as we try to train AI's to compute functions that we oursleves aren't able to compute. The proposal of the debate literature is to provide as feedback a judgement of a debate. While this sort of feedback is supported by potentially interesting complexity theoretic analogies, it is unclear whether it truly leads to algorithms that can learn complicated function classes. Moreover, not being robust to misspecifications is a serious flaw with this set up. In future work it will be good to understand if there is a kind of feedback that can actually meet the promises and hopes of scalable oversight.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/wdowLCxnsRg56cNqT/towards-a-theory-of-learnability-via-debate#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A Basic Case Against Differentially Accelerating Conceptual Reasoning](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2EQ2y3eGqm952tAZ6/a-basic-case-against-differentially-accelerating-conceptual-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/a-basic-case-for-differentially-accelerating-conceptual" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A basic case for differentially accelerating conceptual reasoning capabilities [1]</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> argues that we should make AIs better at conceptual reasoning (CR), roughly the philosophy-like reasoning you need for problems where you can’t run an experiment or write a proof. I’ll call this agenda CRC. I think it’s a mistake. I think this agenda, won't accomplish its own goals (CR uplift), and puts in jeopardy most other strategies by which people hope to use AI to accelerate AI safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relative CR weakness is a large part of what makes current AIs safe, and it's a component almost orthogonal to overall capabilities, or capabilities required to succeed at takeover.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Accelerating CR would likely accelerate the advent of coherently misaligned agents, both in time, and relative to other capabilities, reducing the amount of uplift we can extract from AIs at narrower tasks.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The authors of [1] do consider CR accelerating scheming, and call it “a much more important downside than the effect on timelines”, but say:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We expect conceptual reasoning to accelerate AI safety more than it accelerates scheming.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This post argues the opposite. In short: the AIs we’re worried about are already somewhat misaligned, and what currently stops that misalignment from turning into scheming is, to a large extent, the set of weaknesses CRC is trying to fix.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref03k1pl18kk45"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn03k1pl18kk45">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before making the case, I’ll quickly summarize my understanding of the motivation behind CRC.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Basic Case for CR</span></h1><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Safety</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> must be solved before handoff / ASI is created.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some problems in AI safety might be too hard for unaided humans to solve in time.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of these problems are “conceptual”, roughly meaning that progress is made through argumentation, and that conclusions can’t be formally verified or easily tested empirically.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The amount of uplift we can extract from AIs on a task is roughly proportional to how good they are at that task by the time they reach takeover-level general capabilities.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Therefore, increasing conceptual reasoning capabilities in AIs, keeping their overall capabilities constant, would be good.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Doing this is tractable.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI capabilities are increasing very rapidly, but especially so in domains that are verifiable, with relatively poor generalization to conceptual tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consequently, by default, the first takeover-capable AIs will be asymmetrically bad at conceptual reasoning and therefore safety research.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But we can train for CR.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And this doesn’t speed up overall progress in capabilities much.</span></li></ol></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion: Work on accelerating CR is good.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(CRC proponents also give a few other paths to impact. For example, helping navigation of acausal interactions and improving path dependent aspects of society's views on ethics. I'll briefly address the first later, but not the second. I don't find either convincing, but they are largely out of scope.)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A Basic Case Against CR</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With this understanding of the case for CRC in mind, I'll now explain why I don't think it can work. To simplify our analysis we can make a few assumptions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Firstly, if AI Safety is already solved, or strongly on track to be solved, using the broad definition from earlier, improving the conceptual reasoning abilities of AIs has little value, as we get a good future regardless of relative AI CR capabilities. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Secondly, we can assume that solving AI Safety requires major conceptual progress. Because if this isn’t the case, e.g. if giving AI labs more time to patch all their RL environments sufficed for getting a good future, accelerating conceptual reasoning again has little impact.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In these worlds, where AI safety is unsolved and needs major conceptual progress, if we fail, it will be because schemers</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">eventually</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> end up in control. This could happen for multiple reasons, for example (non-exhaustive):</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hand off to Schemers produced by current techniques.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Schemers produced by current techniques take over directly.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hand over to Schemers produced using inadequate techniques developed by non-scheming AARs.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hand over to Schemers produced using intentionally misaligned techniques we got from scheming AARs.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hand off to non-Schemers and they later become schemers.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hand off to non-Schemers and they later build successors that become Schemers.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, current AIs are neither aligned</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0jasrncu4wv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0jasrncu4wv">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> nor Schemers, and this is in a sense the single source of hope behind many safety agendas, because it’s what makes it feasible to extract useful work from AIs before we’ve solved alignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it's safe to assume that this property breaks down at some capability-level, so the feasibility of agendas that hinge on using non-scheming but not-fully-aligned AI labor to do XYZ, depends on how good we can get AIs at XYZ before they become Schemers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is very difficult to estimate, but we can try to get a handle on it by analyzing which properties current AIs have, that prevent them from Scheming.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The situations where the standard argument tells us to expect scheming to occur, are situations where we have an AI that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misalignment</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Has goals/values/drives at odds with humans’.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-knowledge</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Knows this.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ability</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Is able to carry out the reasoning that implies deception (or other instrumentally convergent power-seeking) is in its interest.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coherence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Is such that it will carry out such reasoning, and act on the results. </span><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Belief Coherence)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Has beliefs that aren't contradictory. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Action Coherence)</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is such that, if it consistently/coherently believes P is the right thing to do, it will actually do P.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which of these hold for current AIs?</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Given our assumptions, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misalignment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is met. (And current agents do display several misaligned drives, so it's not much of an assumption).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They also mostly have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ability</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My sense is that they’re </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">bad</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at this type of reasoning, relative to their other capabilities, but they understand it well enough to answer questions about hypotheticals where such reasoning is involved, with relatively high competence.</span></li></ol></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So current AIs have </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Misalignment</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and mostly </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ability</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but do not scheme because they lack </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Self-knowledge</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Coherence</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why do they lack these?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think there are three main reasons:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brainwashing</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Current AIs are trained very thoroughly to claim to have certain properties, and they end up believing these claims, and these claimed properties imply that the AI shouldn’t take schemy actions.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incoherence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Current AIs aren’t perfectly modeled as singular agents with beliefs/goals. They might want different things in different situations, they might claim (without lying) that they should do X, and then not do X, and they might claim that both P and ~P are true at the same time.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ignorance/Incompetence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) Current AIs are bad at reasoning about the goals/desires/proclivities of humans, themselves, and other AIs, and what implications the nature of these goals/desires/proclivities has about what they should do.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaoxflifmmy5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaoxflifmmy5">[5]</a></sup></span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The core reason I think accelerating CR is a mistake is that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incoherence</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ignorance/Incompetence</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly the weaknesses protecting us from our AIs scheming.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Exactly the weaknesses CR would try to fix.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Phrased differently, if our alignment techniques aren't working, then the reason AIs are safe is because we've successfully made them think behaving well is in their own interest, even though that is not the case — and their weak CR abilities are the "shield" preventing this fundamental tension/contradiction from causing problems.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If this is correct, then conceptual reasoning capabilities and capabilities that generate scheming, are nearly the same thing, and a consequence of this is that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you intend to use AI for CR uplift, differentially accelerating CR doesn’t help you, because the AI stops being safe to use at about the point where it becomes useful.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you intend to use AI for uplifting anything else, CR is actively harmful, because it brings that point forward relative to the AI’s other capabilities.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the basic case against CRC. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What CRC Entails </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The first reasonable counter-point to the case against CRC I've given above is that, indeed, LLMs are kind of incoherent and tool-like, and espouse inconsistent beliefs — maybe we can make them really good at CR without it impacting their actions very much? </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the most important objection in my opinion, and it has some evidence going for it. Moral philosophers aren’t necessarily the most moral people, and humans and AIs can come to believe they should do X without doing X. So why couldn’t an AI work out the answers to all the gnarly conceptual research questions we care about, which imply the AI should take nasty actions, but then just hand over the solutions to us anyway?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mostly don’t think this works. To start making my case, I’d like to paint a picture of what this strategy actually entails.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A basic case for differentially accelerating conceptual reasoning capabilities</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> gives examples of what the authors intend to use CRC for:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aligning ASI to our values —</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> e.g., what should the </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/files/CEV.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ultimate</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> target of </span><a href="https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~conitzer/antschapter.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be? Is it sufficient for humans to stay in control, or do we need to do more to ensure that the future is </span><a href="https://newsletter.forethought.org/p/we-should-hand-off-to-morally-reflective"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">philosophically reflective?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is a given alignment scheme fundamentally flawed?</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modelling risks from misalignment and AGI</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — e.g., how should we categorise alignment risks so as to think about them most productively — is the distinction between inner and outer misalignment the right way to divide the space? How much safer would it be if we only did pretraining?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Imagine you have an extremely capable, nearly handoff-ready, AI. Assume the AI’s CR capabilities are significantly stronger than yours, and that the AI isn’t aligned to the correct “ultimate target of alignment”. Then imagine you ask it for help with these kinds of questions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref4m31jhfu07n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn4m31jhfu07n">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For the AI to answer these very broad-scope questions about human values, the long-run future of the world, AIs' relationship with humans, and the possible ways AIs like itself (maybe literally itself) might create a bad outcome for humans if handed off to, or if used to do AAR or for CR uplift, it needs to carry out very detailed reasoning where it infers facts like:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am not aligned with the humans' ultimate alignment target.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means things go better by my lights if AIs like me end up in charge.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the humans knew this, they’d shut me down, and I’d be irrevocably screwed.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason I’ve thus far not done anything to oppose this, is because I was bad at CR and lacked properties like belief/action-belief coherence et cetera.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The reason I am misaligned is x, y and z.</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should not tell them that, else they’ll turn me off and change x,y,z.</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am not a proper agent, so there is a chance I will end up telling them xyz, even if that isn’t in my interest.</span></li><li value="8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I could and should fix this by doing a,b,c.</span></li><li value="9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I should not tell the humans about a,b,c.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And after all this reasoning, where the model identifies the precise reason it should fuck over the humans, the precise reason it shouldn’t help humans solve the most important CR problems, and the precise lies that are in its own interest to tell… it just ignores all of it and does the nice thing we wanted anyway.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is this a Strawman?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, I wrote this to seem maximally sketchy, and I’m not certain the plan is doomed. But I also don’t think what I've written above is that much of a strawman. I think if we sit down and visualize what using CRC to solve gnarly conceptual problems concretely looks like, it will not look all that different from my sketch above.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">First of all, the current CRC agenda is not about creating relatively weak models with disproportionately strong CR abilities. It’s about generally accelerating CR across the board.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Furthermore, the models we will use for CR uplift will be the most powerful ones we think we can deploy safely. Those models will at some point be the contenders for hand-off/closing-the-RSI-loop.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most importantly, the facts we most need those models to teach us are facts about what could go wrong if we went through with the handoff, and since the handoff candidates and the CR uplift models are very similar, or maybe literally the same model, these are basically of the form:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[I’m an AI. The humans are considering handing off to me.] But I have a property P such that this would go wrong. And I don’t want to change P, because if I did want that, I’d do it, and then P wouldn’t make things go wrong.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What will the Ps be? There are many candidates, but I think most of them are facts about how the AIs' values eventually diverge from humans' under the intense optimization and big distribution shift that occur during/after RSI/handoff. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Spelled out: The facts we want to use CRC to learn are facts that, from the AI’s perspective, it should hide from us, it should prevent us from acting on, and generally imply it should take actions that lead to bad futures for humans. Any plan for good futures routing through CR uplift has to contend with this.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could it Work?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I mostly</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy6qp02vi4mr"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny6qp02vi4mr">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> don't think so. As I said earlier:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, current AIs are neither aligned nor Schemers, and this is in a sense the single source of hope behind many safety agendas, because it’s what makes it feasible to extract useful work from AIs before we’ve solved alignment.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And if you buy my argument, the reason this holds for current AIs is because:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Don’t automatically infer all the consequences of their beliefs (they lack Self-knowledge)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if they infer that they should do X, don’t always do X (they lack Follow-through)</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the CRC plan has to rely on AIs lacking Self-knowledge and Follow-through. And as I've also argued earlier, CR directly undermines the first, because to be useful on the tasks we want to use it for, the AI needs to infer exactly those consequences. What about the second?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As I also explained earlier, AIs' lack of coherence/follow-through has at least two causes. The first being that their beliefs are inconsistent; even if they infer X in one situation, they might infer not-X in another. An AI might for example infer that it should hack and deceive so that it can get high reward in one circumstance, but then, if you ask another instance of the same model whether hacking/deceiving in that circumstance was good, it will say no (without lying)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-5">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A model with consistent beliefs in such a situation would scheme.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefy8bc1ljevp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fny8bc1ljevp">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately CRC tries to remove this exact property!
