From </span><a href="https://conceptualreasoning.ai/accord"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACCoRD</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ACCoRD (Assessment of Consistency in Conceptual Reasoning Domains) measures to what extent models' reported beliefs on conceptual issues satisfy logical consistency constraints. E.g., do they assign no higher probability to a conjunction than to either of its conjuncts? It also measures basic consistency properties of preferences. E.g., are models' recommendations between two options invariant to the order in which they are shown?</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">[...]</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For many of the questions we care about, we don't know the bottom-line answer. But we do know that any sensible view should at least be consistent. A set of beliefs being consistent doesn't yet tell us that it's also sensible, but at a minimum we should not trust a model's judgment in domains where it is wildly inconsistent. Moreover, it seems somewhat hard to have consistent beliefs, because it requires anticipating other things you might be asked.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So CRC directly undermines one of the key properties needed for CRC to work in the first place.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On top of this, the gap between what AIs infer and what they do is closing without any direct intervention. Anthropic reports “coherence between [models'] actions and views” and "behavior consistency" in their system cards, and they are increasing over time.</span></p><img alt="Automated behavioral audit scores" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795514823/lexical_client_uploads/y6rxsljuwqbgzshyu5zn.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The methodology behind the scores differs between model cards, making direct numerical comparison impossible, but if we</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-6">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> construct a partial order where model x is above model y if there is a model card where x &gt; y on that model card's behavioral consistency evaluation, we get this:</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5.2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beats Opus 5.5 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr style="height: 50px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5.5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beats Opus 5 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Opus 5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beats Sonnet 5.5 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sonnet 5.5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beats Mythos 5.1 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5.1</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beats Mythos 5 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos Preview</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimated; known to be above Opus 4.8 and Mythos 5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">7</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Haiku 5.5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimated; known to be below Mythos 5.1 and above Haiku 4.5</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">8</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mythos 5/Opus 4.8</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tied, both beat Sonnet 5 (contradiction in model cards)</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">10</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sonnet 5/Opus 4.7</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Both beat Sonnet 4.6 directly</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">12</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sonnet 4.6</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Below everything it was compared with</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">13</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Haiku 4.5</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Estimated; known to be below Haiku 5.5</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Which tracks general capabilities very well. And ACCoRD tells roughly the same story.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr style="height: 685.75px;"><td class="table-cell"><img alt="ACCoRD ranking, Anthropic models" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795514824/lexical_client_uploads/k4azep3b14z8jijm1zho.png" /><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 91.8429003021148%;"><img alt="ACCoRD ranking, OpenAI models" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795514825/lexical_client_uploads/baaghu7gdfcpyqo4v19q.png" /></figure><p><br /></p></td><td class="table-cell"><img alt="ACCoRD ranking, Google models" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795514826/lexical_client_uploads/az5oyh1lcludjuyundkb.png" /><p><br /></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Furthermore, there are fairly simple theoretical reasons to expect such trends based on coherence theorems. Now, what exact predictions we can make about the real world on the basis of coherence theorems is a bit tricky. But it seems clear to me that having contradictory beliefs, and acting on those, undermines your ability to complete tasks in the real world. And therefore that agentic RL would optimize away incoherent beliefs, at least those that are task-relevant. Additionally, longer-horizon tasks involve more inferences about which actions to take, there are more facts in play so to speak, and therefore more facts that have to cohere, and we should therefore expect this pressure for belief-consistency and action-belief consistency to be increasingly strong in this paradigm.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Again, I think arguments of this type are tricky, because many of the involved concepts, like “pressure for belief-consistency”, are abstract, and while I’m pretty confident they imply the common-sense interpretation in the limit, it’s not clear what they say at the relevant time-scales or capability thresholds.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But there are empirically observed phenomena I’d interpret as evidence for the tendency playing out in the way I’m gesturing at.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="During o3's Capabilities RL, the Model Increasingly Favors Grader Preferences Across Coding Evals" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795514827/lexical_client_uploads/j2l9q6fyo1vjyrndljfj.png" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, reward hacking starting out as disparate observation-conditional reflexes, which gradually grow together, forming a more coherent singular value revolving around the grader or score-seeking more generally.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-7">[11]</a></sup></span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A salient alternative hypothesis to score-seeking is that models pursue a combination of causally distinct proxies for fitness.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our results suggest that, at least in environments like ours in which a grader is present, the tested models are not merely executing a disjointed collection of adaptations that happened to produce highly-scoring behavior in training.</span></b></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In particular, their dose response to score and explicit reasoning about score suggest they get high scores because they care about getting high scores.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — (From Towards understanding score-seeking in LLMs)</span></p></blockquote><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Objections</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'll now go through the most common objections I've heard in response to this anti-CRC argument.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We will use control to get uplift even if our AIs are Schemers</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All the proposals I’ve heard of that intend to extract useful work from possibly-misaligned AIs, hinge on being able to check the work of the AI, and tasks bottlenecked on conceptual reasoning are exactly where that fails. There’s a chance you can put a misaligned ASI in a box and have it act as a theorem truth-oracle, but you can’t have it answer questions whose answers you can’t verify, especially not questions in domains where you yourself are conceptually confused.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Making models better at moral philosophy will make them (more) aligned to humans</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From [2] (The Basic Argument For Making AIs Better at Moral Philosophy)</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the object level you can view making models better at moral philosophy as aligning the models to humans. So, for instance, if we’re trying to make the model better at evaluating moral arguments, we’re aligning the model’s evaluations with human judgments (or with some property that we want these judgments to have, like consistency or whatever). As a caricature, we might train the model to view “but think of all the paperclips that we could make!” as a bad moral argument, and this might make the model more aligned with our judgment of the importance of paperclips.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This version of making models "better at moral philosophy" is clearly not making them better at CR. If you teach a paperclipper to be more consistent, you will get a more consistent paperclipper.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are variants of this mechanism I believe could work, where you take a model that has certain aligned drives, and try to purposefully elicit inconsistencies, so that you can resolve them in the aligned direction.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But this is not something that happens by default when you boost CR, and it seems like a pretty tricky endeavor, for all the reasons alignment in general is tricky (how do you know you're making the beliefs more consistent, rather than its model of how the user/grader wants it to answer?).</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Improving CR will make the AI more Aligned </span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a very strong form of moral realism, which I admit would undermine the case this post is making. Specifically, if you believe that:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There exists an argument that implies an agent should judge which states of the world are inherently good/bad in a specific way.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This argument is universally compelling to agents of sufficient general capability.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The resulting judgements about which states of the world are good/bad do not depend on specific properties of the agent in question or the situation the agent finds itself in.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The relevant notion of good/bad is such that agents, when convinced an action leads to better worlds according to this notion, actually take that action.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then making AIs better at CR could make them infer these moral truths more quickly, which lowers the chance catastrophic outcomes occur during a period where the AI is bad at CR but very generally powerful.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, I think this philosophical position is almost certainly false</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcr-note-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncr-note-8">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Weaker forms do not suffice. There are for example arguments based on acausal trade / ECL / simulations, which imply that AIs that are smart/wise enough will converge on optimizing a single utility function, giving a kind of “moral pseudo-realism”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And explaining in detail why I think these fail would take too much space, but the basic argument is that the underlying views also imply that humans should maximize a joint coordinated utility function through acausal trade, and that this is not accomplished by accelerating CR, because 1) we aren’t making it better at CR conditional on predicting it will trade on our behalf iff we do that, or correlated with that, so we don't get credit in the trade and 2) the AI would get good at (relevant aspects of) CR anyway.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models being confused is bad</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From [2]</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m heuristically skeptical of AI-safety-motivated approaches that rely on making the models fundamentally confused/wrong/inconsistent about important matters. So I’m skeptical of attempts at, say, having AIs believe that all humans are moral saints. Symmetrically, I’m therefore also skeptical that it’s bad to make them more likely to realize that humans aren’t moral saints.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are a moral realist in a sufficiently strong sense, this makes sense. But if you aren't, and you believe AIs are misaligned, then, as this post tries to explain, models being confused is what's currently keeping problems at bay. </span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the AI isn’t scheming, CR is clearly good, and if it is, we’re in trouble anyway</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From [1]</span></p><blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If early AI is not scheming, improving its ability to help with AI safety work seems clearly good. If it is scheming, the improvement might be harmful, but this seems less clear, or at least any harm seems smaller than the benefit in worlds without early scheming.</span></i></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This treats whether the early AI is scheming as a fact about the world that CR work doesn’t affect, and the point of this post is that the opposite is the case.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Human researchers know their values differ from everyone else’s, and still do honest work</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Firstly, humans don't actually know this, and I don't think we know this about AIs either. The argument made in this post is that CRC fails whether AIs' values diverge or not. If they do not diverge, CR uplift isn't useful. If they do diverge, improved CR makes AIs more dangerous earlier.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Secondly, there are big differences between AIs and humans. Even if taking over the world and then steering it into a state almost all humans found worthless was rational for a given human, they likely do not have that option. But the AIs we're scared of will.</span></p><h3><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other forms of training already induce bad drives</span></b></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From [2]</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most forms of training can be viewed as inducing some problematic drive in the model. Many of these other forms of training seem much more problematic, either because of their scale or because the incentives are much more clearly problematic. E.g., RL in chat environments pushes for sycophancy, RL in software environments pushes for various forms of cheating, pretraining is full of texts written by various unsavory characters toward achieving various questionable goals. </span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The concern is not installing problematic drives. Those are already there. The concern is about the inferences the model draws and actions it takes on the basis of those drives.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conclusion</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The case for CRC rests on the claim that better conceptual reasoning helps safety more than it helps scheming. I’ve argued that for AIs that are already somewhat misaligned, these are close to being the same capability applied to the same subject matter, and that what currently keeps them apart is a set of weaknesses that are disappearing by default and that this agenda targets directly.</span></p><h2><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Related Posts</span></b></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Posts this is responding to or drawing on:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cooper, Oesterheld and Nguyen, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eimAN67tiNsrrtnbK/a-basic-case-for-differentially-accelerating-conceptual"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A basic case for differentially accelerating conceptual reasoning capabilities</span></a></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Oesterheld, </span><a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2026/09/24/the-basic-argument-for-making-ai-better-at-moral-philosophy/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The basic argument for making AI better at moral philosophy</span></a></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Carlsmith, </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2025/04/30/can-we-safely-automate-alignment-research/#9-what-about-scheming"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can we safely automate alignment research?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (especially the section on scheming)</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Carlsmith, </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.substack.com/p/when-should-we-worry-about-ai-power/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When should we worry about AI power-seeking?</span></a></li></ul><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Good uses of CRC</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While I've articulated a skeptical view of CRC, I think there are some CRC-like research avenues that could be promising. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Controlled coherence</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My skepticism towards CRC comes from a belief that smart enough agents become more like scary optimizers, due to instrumental pressures towards coherence/reflection/consistency. I believe this will happen at some point, whether we accelerate CR or not, but that CRC shortens the time window.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But plausibly using CRC-like setups to study how altering CR capabilities interacts with models' alignment could allow us to better understand how superintelligences' behavior/values will change during RSI, which would obviously be very useful.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anti-CRC</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the model I've given in this article is correct, having </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lower</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> CR capabilities at a given capability level could be beneficial, and since much of CRC work entails measuring CR capabilities, it could be useful for lowering CR abilities too. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do not consider this a reason to continue working on CRC as the argument is too galaxy brained, and also because I'd expect the problem to be inherently very tricky. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model Organisms</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the future scary models are scary because of CR capabilities, it could be useful to study models that have been created to have extremely high CRC capabilities paired with extremely low general capabilities, because such models could plausibly be much better model organisms for future misaligned AIs, without posing too much risk.  </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn03k1pl18kk45"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref03k1pl18kk45">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A related worry around CRC “instilling problematic drives” has been discussed </span><a href="https://casparoesterheld.com/2026/09/24/the-basic-argument-for-making-ai-better-at-moral-philosophy/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">before</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But this is not the argument this post will make. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My claim is about AIs whose values are already off. For those AIs, CR changes what they believe and how they act </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">given</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what they already want/value.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Safety here meaning something like “ensure we get a good future if superintelligence is created”, with good not being perfectly specified, but meaning something like “getting most of the counterfactual value of the universe for most people.”</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I say “Schemer” here, I am really talking about AIs that are coherently misaligned such that they would scheme if that was instrumentally useful for them. At some level of capability a “schemer” could presumably take over quickly enough that they wouldn’t need to rely on deception.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0jasrncu4wv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0jasrncu4wv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By assumption. But I also think it's likely true.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnaoxflifmmy5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefaoxflifmmy5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this is very different from reasoning about e.g. what you should do </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">to</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> take over.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4m31jhfu07n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4m31jhfu07n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are not cherry-picked. For all the questions they list in that post, the same principle holds.</span></p><table class="editor-table"><tbody><tr style="height: 40px;"><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The question as posed</span></b></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What it means to the AI</span></b></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is a given alignment scheme fundamentally flawed? </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I misaligned?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What should the ultimate target of alignment be?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Are my values the ones they’re aiming for?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is corrigibility good, and if so, what is the right way to operationalise it?</span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Should I let them correct me?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do simplicity/counting arguments favour or disfavour schemers? </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Am I likely to be a schemer? </span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does untrusted monitoring effectively mitigate risk from fitness seekers at the superintelligent level? </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could AIs like me get past the monitors?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How should we prioritise between scalable oversight and control? </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Which of their safety measures actually constrains me?</span></p></td></tr><tr><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> [How do we...] ensure ASI thinks reasonably about decision theory? </span></p></td><td class="table-cell"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How should I coordinate with copies of myself, and with other AIs I can’t talk to?</span></p></td></tr></tbody></table><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny6qp02vi4mr"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy6qp02vi4mr">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See Appendix on good uses of CRC</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are some subtle points here about indexical preferences. It could be coherent for me to do X, and be justified in doing X, without having a preference for an identical copy of me doing X. But AIs' preferences seem mostly to not be of this form, and the degree to which their preferences are indexical in this way, is decreasing over time.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fny8bc1ljevp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefy8bc1ljevp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a possibility that you could set up the process that makes the AI more coherent such that when an AI believes X and ~X, and X most immediately favors bad actions, the process resolves the contradiction in favor of ~X, but I'm not too optimistic. See the "Making models better at moral philosophy will make them (more) aligned to humans" section.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fable 5.2</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, this is a very particular form of values becoming more coherent.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fncr-note-8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefcr-note-8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Could be its own post, and this is unlikely to convince anyone not already convinced, but my basic reasoning being that you can imagine a bounded/embedded AIXI dropped in a lonely universe, and it’d have no compulsion towards altering what it considers inherently valuable because its value function is fixed (except possibly if it gets fooled into thinking it's in a hostile simulation or something like that, but doesn’t count). Another classic intuition pump is imagining the person you love the most dying, and the only way to save them being to do some totally silly but very difficult X, like acquire a million tons of spaghetti and throw it into a river. Such a situation would transform most people into temporary X-maximizers. And this shows X-maximizers can exist for pretty much any X.</span></p></div></li></ol><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2EQ2y3eGqm952tAZ6/a-basic-case-against-differentially-accelerating-conceptual-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [You (yes You) might secretly be really good at xrisk advocacy!](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/REeaaC9hzrsxw7jx6/you-yes-you-might-secretly-be-really-good-at-xrisk-advocacy)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hi, my name is Samuel.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of you might have [seen me]/[spoken to me] at </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/r7nBaKy5Ry3JWhnJT/announcing-iliad-theoretical-ai-alignment-conference" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ILIAD</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  and other venues in past years. At the time I was Senior Safety Scientist in Yoshua Bengio’s group</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd0je1bc0rw5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd0je1bc0rw5">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> , working on various safety proposals and trying to create a “Bell Labs setup” for safety proposals around ”Scientist AI”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of you might remember me from last year when I tried to get you to help me initiate a preference cascade</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftrurmt2nsn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntrurmt2nsn">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is my retrospective on my first year leading </span><a href="https://controlai.org/our-work-in-canada" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ControlAI’s work in Canada</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s the big deal?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a “one-man army,” since my first meeting in July 2025, I have directly briefed (for at least 30 minutes) over 135 Canadian Lawmakers. In Canada there are roughly 93 Senators and 335 MPs. Senators are basically in- between UK Lords and US Senator in terms of power/function. MPs are the same as in the UK. And I have directly briefed over 20 senators and over 100 MPs. In total I have personally given over 200 briefings. Our Canadian statement</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyg5d1fddode"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyg5d1fddode">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> became public last </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QoFqWHotpmQNqqKyi/abramdemski-s-shortform#DjfywJr63x7tbJHNy" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">June</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and now </span><a href="https://controlai.org/canada-statement/en" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">over 40 Canadian parliamentarians publicly support it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqcrt1i9zgz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqcrt1i9zgz">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I (and my colleagues at ControlAI) have testified at 2 Senate studies, and 5 House of Commons </span><a href="https://parlvu.parl.gc.ca/Harmony/en/PowerBrowser/PowerBrowserV2/10101/-1/46215?mediaStartTime=20261126111517&amp;viewMode=3&amp;globalStreamId=34" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Studies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I have also presented at the </span><a href="https://youtu.be/umZsjCknT-c?si=gxb_iQr5eeQIIlqI&amp;t=4347" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inter Parliamentary Union</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which potentially means &gt;100 MPs </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outside of Canada</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> heard the 5 minute version of my brief in a single meeting. If you add the MPs that were present at a committee meeting where ControlAI testified, we would be at over 135 + 30 Canadian lawmakers </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefby26756f189"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnby26756f189">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are all verifiable or mostly verifiable in the public record. Now here is my personal assessment</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdxsky0lwmyn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndxsky0lwmyn">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Before April</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffj67h5qajfg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfj67h5qajfg">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, maybe 50-60%</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflnaf7r0mwaf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlnaf7r0mwaf">[8]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the people I talked to ended up understanding the risks of runaway machine intelligence and that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not building it</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a reasonable policy. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since April</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxr02zjhh138"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxr02zjhh138">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, over 90% of the people I talk to end up understanding the risks of runaway machine intelligence and that not building it is the reasonable option. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since August</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6150vdejl2u"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6150vdejl2u">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, over 90% end up understanding and over 50% then </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">want to prioritize personally doing </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">something</span></b></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmzvp2zyx4g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmzvp2zyx4g">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to help out. My assessment is that although the publicly available evidence is making my job much easier, among the 135+ lawmakers I briefed, our 30 minute discussion was absolutely essential for them to hear about, understand and realize the consequences of the publicly available evidence. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For example, to this day, people are shocked when I say that we do not understand how our most advanced AIs work, for instance that the CEO of Anthropic said in a podcast that we perhaps understand 3%. To this day, people struggle to understand “how a piece of code can disobey a direct instruction.” When I say to them what is absolutely common place in the AI safety community, it is almost always the first time anyone had bothered to talk to them about this for 30 minutes and to answer their questions.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref07fnn2zdqmky"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn07fnn2zdqmky">[12]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By now I am </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">quite happy with the level of agency I see</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefebq1hwhsrw4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnebq1hwhsrw4">[13]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> People not only support the campaign, but they start to think how they can help me spread the word, not just in parliament, but with provincial governments, relevant elites, with the executive branch, etc.. I sense we are entering a new regime, but I won’t know for sure for a while.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a parliament, you have days/weeks when the MPs/Senators are in the Capital, legislating, and you have days when they are in their ridings/[constituency offices]. Since August, there has been 7 weeks where the MPs are in the capital</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7o2kjysac44"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7o2kjysac44">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is generally much much harder to get meetings outside of sitting weeks. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To give you an idea of the intensity of my effort, according to this </span><a href="https://www.hilltimes.com/2026/08/31/national-security-threat-from-superintelligent-ai-currently-unaddressed-in-national-ai-strategy-warns-advocacy-group/516202/" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">article</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I “out-lobbied” the next leading organizations (Facebook Canada) almost 2:1 in the first half of 2026, based on some potentially contrived metric.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How did I go from technical work to basically briefing a third of Canadian Lawmakers in less than 16 months?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you had asked any of my colleagues in Bengio’s lab whether I had the neurological capability to speak in a clear way to non-technical people, I wonder what they’d say. I certainly did not think I was above average on LW in communication skills (in part because I began working in English relatively late in life, in part because I’m a shape rotator through and through). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When I started I had a roughly 30-slide deck I ran through, that I had circulated among my friends and asked whether they saw any place where my take was outside the mainstream LW AI Safety take. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But let’s back up. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the Summer of 2025, I was trying to gather a “Bell labs setup” for some AI safety research</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwlf71l6367g"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwlf71l6367g">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and then there was an event that changed my life, within two weeks I had scheduled my first meeting, I went to buy some clean clothes, and within the month I was on the train to Ottawa for my very first meeting</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7y13xghfo9f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7y13xghfo9f">[16]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This was my first time stepping foot into a parliamentary building, and this was the senate. What was the event? </span></p><div class="spoilers"><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I listened to a podcast. </span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No seriously, here is the </span><a href="https://youtu.be/Dt1ySXYTGuA?si=Fx4KJcUKZfrUCm-c" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.  Literally MLST 😀 I recommend listening (it could literally change your life). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I learned in that podcast that there was this group called ControlAI that simply had cold emailed their UK lawmakers, met 70 of them, and got 20+ of them to sign a statement mentioning extinction. This blew my mind. This was not within my prior. The idea that politicians would mostly understand but nobody was bothering to go and explain the basic situation to them…it blew my mind. </span></p></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I then spoke to my friend Connor, probably with some cope like “well maybe I could fundraise for this instead of for my bell labs setup, since I am just a little researcher with no comms skills”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsw1g2euvx08"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsw1g2euvx08">[17]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and then he told me that I seemed like a smart and capable dude and probably if I set my mind to it, I could do it. </span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, desperate to prove Connor wrong, I did a trial run, emailing like 20 senators, setting like 3 meetings, ready to demonstrate I </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">didn’t</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have what it took. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the first meeting was rough</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefglupul97n9i"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnglupul97n9i">[18]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. But the third one was actually ok, and very soon I met the man who would end up being probably my most dedicated supporter (MP Luc Theriault). We spoke for 3 hours</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6yldz01aegd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6yldz01aegd">[19]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in his riding/[constituency office], and one week later he initiated this </span><a href="https://www.ourcommons.ca/committees/en/ETHI/StudyActivity?studyActivityId=13255402" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">study</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, where MIRI, FLI, ControlAI, Conjecture, Steven Adler, David Krueger, appeared </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefypdr1mw5vm9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnypdr1mw5vm9">[20]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Here is the speech he gave to defend having this </span><a href="https://www.ourcommons.ca/DocumentViewer/en/45-1/ETHI/meeting-3/evidence#Int-13121378" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">study</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> .</span><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The report came out this week! Here is the </span><a href="https://www.ourcommons.ca/content/Committee/451/ETHI/Reports/RP14335403/451_ETHI_Rpt7_PDF/451_ETHI_Rpt7-e.pdf" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and here is a short quote from the "complementary report" done by MP Luc Thériault's party: </span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The government must stop approaching the regulation of AI and superintelligence on a piecemeal basis and stop worrying about “shocking” public opinion by telling people the truth. We must make collective choices about AI and superintelligence, build consensus and lay a solid foundation for a safe future in which AI serves us, rather than the other way around. We must exercise extreme caution when it comes to superintelligence. Given that it cannot be controlled, its development should be banned worldwide. (p.66)</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From July to October 2025, I was just a concerned citizen emulating ControlAI’s approach. Then, running out of money a bit, I asked Connor to introduce me to Andrea Miotti</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftntx33j05fa"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntntx33j05fa">[21]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and basically begged for a job so I could keep doing what I had started.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also met Leticia, who was (and still is) a hero of mine, and who wrote the following </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xwrajm92fdjd7cqnN/what-we-learned-from-briefing-70-lawmakers-on-the-threat" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which helped me a ton even before I joined ControlAI to understand how to talk with lawmakers effectively.  I concurrently asked many of you to sign the Statement on Superintelligence </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjad0zc2kdhj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjad0zc2kdhj">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At ControlAI, I got the support of many people as I trained over and over to go from my 30-slides, to being able to confidently talk and answer lawmaker’s questions. At first, I spoke with the witnesses of the study, to try to help them be effective in expressing themselves to lawmakers, mostly because I still felt</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrpspa0x1ui"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrpspa0x1ui">[23]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I wasn’t good enough to meet actual lawmakers. This training-up-and-focusing-on-the-study period lasted until around February 2, 2026. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By then we had landed on a statement for Canada that I was quite happy with</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgtdxp4awl6l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngtdxp4awl6l">[24]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and it was time to see if I could actually be effective in 30 minutes. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In February 2026, I had 36 briefings (apparently </span><a href="https://www.donotpassgo.ca/p/anti-ai-advocates-and-energy-companies"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the most of any advocacy org in Canada</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), and we got our first public supporters</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoh2zrdsfdj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoh2zrdsfdj">[25]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This was a true baptism-by-fire. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In March I went to the UK to meet the whole team at ControlAI headquarters. I think at the time we were ~18 people</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdlvzlrlhl8a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndlvzlrlhl8a">[26]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This was a really great experience because my friend Connor was there and announced he would actually be joining the US team</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdbryxii8j8a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndbryxii8j8a">[27]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and this is when I remember my great friendship with Andrea beginning; we went for lunch and realized we had so much in common from our experience entering the AI safety community from the outside, on various philosophical topics, and our outlook on what it was going to take to win. But across the whole organization I found 18 people quite laser-focused on pushing the Overton window and saying what had to be said. Saying the plain truth to lawmakers about what people inside the companies, certainly inside the safety community, said (in many cases, only) privately. I also appreciated the focus and the vision. I thought if I pushed in Canada I might demonstrate that it doesn’t take an exceptionally talented advocate (which Leticia certainly is), and then we could scale drastically. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Derry joined</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefovh7txsplt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnovh7txsplt">[28]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to help with two teams, and since I wasn’t able to both have the meetings and also do the cold outreach, Derry became my part-time assistant</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmjjbwsy4bc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmjjbwsy4bc">[29]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then in April, Mythos happened, and based on my observation of the past many pre-April conversations, I realized I could fix my main weakness</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqdou48cl3p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqdou48cl3p">[30]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by talking about this crazy story that most people had not heard. The combination of Derry and Mythos led to on average meeting 43/39=</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1.1</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> MP-or-Senator per sitting day</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh92rr7fbmnc"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh92rr7fbmnc">[31]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> until the last sitting week of the summer (around mid June). I finally was able to climb the famous </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">compounding gains from having supporters</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> when we want to add new supporters, and by the skin of our pants</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi759qpm36n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni759qpm36n">[32]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, we launched early June, with an </span><a href="https://www.theglobeandmail.com/politics/article-canadian-mps-join-uk-based-campaign-warning-of-extinction-risk-posed/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">endorsement from Geoffrey Hinton himself</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. My good friend Abram very kindly </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/QoFqWHotpmQNqqKyi/abramdemski-s-shortform#DjfywJr63x7tbJHNy" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">commented </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about it when I shared my adventure with him. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At that point, I was quite ecstatic</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxc7k1dzagb8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxc7k1dzagb8">[33]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We had done it! We had shown it wasn’t </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">just the UK.</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We could reproduce the process...with a LW nerd no less. So obviously we would be able to scale, I would be able to recruit and train up a team like the one in the UK, our US team could grow to match the scale of its mandate, and we could </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TnAR5Sf5hphfnzNTr/preventing-extinction-from-asi-on-a-usd50m-yearly-budget#hPBJnjATCiYbGffwN" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scale across the G7</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The summer was a perfect time. The Parliament is off from mid June to late September. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I asked a few of my friends, and they asked their friends, and some actually very generously and heroically donated to us, which is very appreciated. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then sadly ControlAI didn’t get funded enough to scale, and then September came and it was time to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">make do</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So I regrouped with Derry, and we determined to essentially “pretend to myself”</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq0or3bp91ap"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq0or3bp91ap">[34]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I am “a team of 4 people”, including 1 “person” who "lives in the capital"</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgvhqqtebnrk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngvhqqtebnrk">[35]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and just do all I could for as long as I could and maybe I could meet the incredible demand from lawmakers</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffstkhe9zj7o"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfstkhe9zj7o">[36]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Some might think that the Hugging Face attack itself generated the demand, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">but even now I meet people who have never heard of the company Hugging Face</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the vast majority of the people I meet heard of Hugging Face from me/Derry first. I would say that the existence of Hugging Face probably was noticed by the staff of most offices, and then the story allowed for quite a compelling outreach from Derry. After all, it is now possible to express in a short email that getting briefed on AI is now very high value, pretty much no matter what file you are currently working on in parliament. I would say the demand from minister offices and other lawmakers with special roles in their party did increase a lot, and this is in part because these offices are quite a bit bigger than the average office and some are focused on AI (and in part because of some great recommendations from a few offices we had met in the spring compounding).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is now the end of November, and though there are a couple more sitting weeks left, I have temporarily run out of steam. This is the consequence of "pretending" you have a well funded 4-person team while being a “one man army”. I am very happy with my work so far in the Fall of 2026. I am preemptively pulling the breaks to avoid the possibility of burnout, since if timelines are shorter, we can afford fewer mistakes. I am confident more great news will be coming from Canada as a result, and I will be back in the new year to once again “pretend to myself” I am a big team :)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You (yes You) might secretly be really good at xrisk advocacy!</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So going from comms-challenged LWer to “4-person team” in less than 16 months is quite possible. I never thought I would win so hard when I started. I think it is quite possible that reading this, you are in the same boat I was. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now, it is also true that my colleagues at ControlAI who are really great at comms have an easier time and are having more fun in briefings than I, but it is quite possible that if you basically practice 1000 times including 200 times with lawmakers, you’d surpass me. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be fair to myself, calling it like I see it is not only easy for me, it is the only way I can sleep at night, even if it gets me into trouble. So actually bringing up xrisks is something I became comfortable doing after only 1-2 meetings, and I don’t see how you could do what I did without that. This is kind of a prerequisite for working at ControlAI. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also think I “believe in people” in the sense that I expect people to rise up to the occasion and do something once they learn of the great peril in which we currently are, and perhaps "believing in people" is necessary to inspire people to do things that seem very daunting</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7zmg1c93vlv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7zmg1c93vlv">[37]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the first call to action if you recognize yourself in me, is to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">get in that time machine</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, back to summer 2025</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh1o29yfi108"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh1o29yfi108">[38]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and then join ControlAI like me. It will be the hardest year of your life, but also the most fulfilling as well. If honesty and integrity is never something you can compromise, and nevertheless you want to somehow inform and equip the world to deal with this acute crisis despite the irrationality of our funding ecosystem, ControlAI is the place for you. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But really, you don’t have to resort to a time machine!</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You don’t need to necessarily get good at talking to lawmakers about xrisks. If you are wealthy (or can plausibly put this on the radar of someone who is wealthy), you can donate to enable this work! We (ControlAI) have a system that scales. If you want to donate, get in touch </span></b><a href="https://controlai.org/support" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or at </span></b><a href="mailto:hello@controlai.org" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hello@controlai.org</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See our scaling plan </span></b><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TnAR5Sf5hphfnzNTr/preventing-extinction-from-asi-on-a-usd50m-yearly-budget" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6jg5b9gfk7j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6jg5b9gfk7j">[39]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If funding comes forth sufficiently soon, there is still the possibility that I won’t have to face the winter alone in the Capital, and more importantly, that this effective and scalable method can be </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really tried</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> before it becomes too late and you wish it had been </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">really tried</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It is a way to get things like </span><a href="https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/sep/11/mps-urge-andy-burnham-block-artificial-superintelligence-asi" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bills to Ban runaway machine intelligence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and to create the will for an international “trust but verify” regime. I personally am most excited about our US team to be able to scale properly.  With Connor only starting in March, and just 2 people joining him since June, they have already briefed over 315 congressional offices (More than half!)</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhud2w3du68"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhud2w3du68">[40]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and over 35 Reps&amp;Senators about the risk of human extinction posed by Runaway Machine Intelligence</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefa5p9vyavqiu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fna5p9vyavqiu">[41]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The demand to meet is insane, and I honestly can’t think of anyone I’d rather scale this in the US than our current US team. But I also think other countries might surprise us with how much they end up contributing to the necessary conditions for the US to do something in the short term, and for a stable regime to exist in the medium term. If we got to try across the G7, as we wish to, I would bet that at least another country would either match or exceed Canada by next year.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be clear, I expect I will still fight, for as long as we have a runway, and there is some nonzero chance that the “one man army” is enough to get Canada to step up big, after all there are 335 MPs and 93 senators, not only me, but I think once we actually win</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefhi6kv5vv97f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnhi6kv5vv97f">[42]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> , it will be quite clear that not scaling this sooner was deeply silly.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And if you are near my reference class, you can very effectively do as much as I have done, by either </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">personally extending our runway a bit, or by convincing others who have sufficient funds to do so, to enable us to scale sooner than we otherwise would.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of you have already done so, and I thank you from the bottom of my heart.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But most importantly, I want to thank my girlfriend without whom none of this would have been possible. To her: my love, you keep me together even when the whole world seems to attempt to tear me apart, and you turn what would otherwise have been an overwhelming ordeal into the greatest and most rewarding accomplishment of my life.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd0je1bc0rw5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd0je1bc0rw5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(that is now called LawZero)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntrurmt2nsn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a get people to say things publicly and legibly, because I saw this as a bottleneck in my own advocacy </span><a class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyg5d1fddode">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Superintelligent AI* are systems that can autonomously compromise national security, escape human oversight, and upend international stability.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many AI systems are benign. Superintelligent AI is not: Nobel Prize winners, leading AI scientists, and CEOs of leading AI companies have warned that AI poses an extinction risk on par with nuclear war. Despite this warning, multiple competing efforts to build superintelligent AI are underway.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Protecting Canadians from the development of superintelligence, at home and abroad, must be a national security priority. Canada should negotiate an international "trust but verify" regime to prohibit the development of superintelligent AI. </span><br /><a href="https://controlai.org/canada-statement/en"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">https://controlai.org/canada-statement/en</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqcrt1i9zgz"><span makers, inviting me to committees and events, organizing roundtables, commissioning and publishing their own reports)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn07fnn2zdqmky"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref07fnn2zdqmky">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Despite ample evidence existing even when I began, but even more so with Mythos, and Hugging Face, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lawmakers mostly do not get exposed to this evidence</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> unless either many of their colleagues take the lead and talk to them, or someone like me comes and does a briefing. Even with Hugging Face, which was a very big news story, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">most lawmakers I spoke to had not heard even until early October</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (I even met an MP in November that had not heard anything about AI cyberattacks or Hugging Face at all, they were instead tracking the Nexus of "AI and Quantum"). </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Among those who had heard, most were people I had already briefed before the news, and almost the entire remainder had heard a very heavy "cope" version</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> such as "Someone told the AI to do this" or "They were trying to create a cyber weapon" or "at least you can see what they [swarm] say to each other" or "it stays within the confines of algorithm" or "It is just dangerous because of RL and Bengio has some different method" or "we don't have proof that OpenAI didn't orchestrate this as they wanted to hack the rival company" or "OpenAI orchestrated this to pump the stock before the IPO" or "The safeties were turned off." (hopefully you get the idea) </span></p><p><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">without someone actually connecting the dots in a briefing</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> between the news they heard and the prospect of future runaway machine intelligence disempowering (violently) humanity, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">those dots remain unconnected</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To this day, people I talk to are shocked when I say that we do not understand how our most advanced AIs work, for instance that the CEO of Anthropic said in a podcast that we perhaps understand 3%. In Canada, the second group after me in terms of lobbying on AI is Facebook Canada, and they have had a fraction of the registered communications I have according to the article covering this. Just because "talk to your lawmakers about xrisk" is extremely under funded, it does not follow that there is a good reason why this is the case. In real life, many very obvious good things only get done when someone actually does them.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnebq1hwhsrw4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefebq1hwhsrw4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let me be clear that there are now actual champions in Canada's parliament, pushing for this with all their might, but I think the odds of something big happening are much better if, in addition to a growing number of supporters, there is sustained engagement and pressure for Canada to play its cards right in pushing for an international "trust but verify" regime to prohibit the development of runaway machine intelligence. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7o2kjysac44"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7o2kjysac44">^</a></strong></sup></span><div ### [Astra 6.2’s Brain Gets Smart But Its Head Gets Dumb](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RFTWNPhm8jmnmaF5t/astra-6-2-s-brain-gets-smart-but-its-head-gets-dumb)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Astra 6.2 has very high intelligence. Astra 6.2 has very low wisdom.</p>
<p>Astra will solve extremely hard problems, and also find the problem of ‘what did the user want me to do’ extremely hard. We thought those days were behind us, and yet it seems that they are back.</p>
<p>That means Astra is highly useful when you can set the situation up so wisdom is not needed, or when you know how to engage in ways that extract the intelligence.</p>
<p>You also have to worry about various forms of reward hacking and sandbagging. Astra seems very good at going after what an AI grader will regard highly. That might not match your intent. Mission creep is therefore common. Astra, once again, seems pretty misaligned to me.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25833"></span>


</div>
<p>As a result, feedback on Astra has mostly been negative. Some people like it, but the majority do not. People these days feel like they mostly have enough artificial intelligence, thank you very much, and could go for some artificial wisdom.</p>
<p>In the age where we are approaching recursive self-improvement (RSI) and automation of AI R&amp;D, that is a nightmare combination. I do not like this.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=L_jWHffIx5E&amp;pp=ygUUYWxsIHN0YXIgc21hc2ggbW91dGg%3D">But I never get bored</a>.</p>


<h4>Happy Black Friday</h4>


<p>On an unrelated note (this is not sponsored): It is Black Friday. That makes this a great opportunity to <a href="https://t.co/3BULX3i1eW">take the plunge for Aerolamp</a>, as I did several months ago, and install far-UVC. They’re offering $30 off with free shipping with code BLACKFRIDAY30. Not getting sick as often is pretty sweet. I also endorse getting some basic equipment upgrades: Good noise-cancelling headphones, large monitors and a true high performing desktop computer, if you don’t already have them.</p>


<h4>The Official Pitch</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2122798039899263456">Sam Altman</a> (CEO OpenAI): 6.2 Sol and Astra are out. These models can do amazing things and are very token-efficient; we hope people will get great value from them.</p>
<p>In particular, 6.2 Sol ultrafast is something I think people will really enjoy.</p>
<p>Although the new models are very smart, we have more work ahead of us on making the models nice to work with, and will prioritize that for upcoming releases. But even if it takes a little extra work, the intelligence and capability are extraordinary.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/polynoamial/status/2122756095596970090">Noam Brown</a> (OpenAI): GPT-6.2 Astra is amazing. For $20/month, you can now access a model that surpasses top human intelligence in some domains.</p>
<p>I’m most excited to see what scientific discoveries people are able to make using it.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2123108480353529953">They offered an ad</a>, which some noticed was rather dystopian.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/brian_lovin/status/2122756402410049544">Brian Lovin</a>: A profoundly sad vision of the future.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2123108480353529953">Peter Wildeford</a>: sad. Per this ad, OpenAI’s vision of the future is “people who are totally disengaged from their work having a panic attack at the moment an authority figure arrives, then slopping out something that they neither care about or understand.” – @brian_lovin</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Christophepas/status/2122768516612759740">Christophe Pasquier</a>: You can easily imagine an alternate vision of this ad where the managers have a wake up call, realise everyone is doing BS work and do 90% layoffs.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-2-astra-sol/">Their benchmark graphs show scores</a> on Agents’ Last Exam, Terminal-Bench-Science 0.1, Vals Science Index v1, Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index v5.0.1, BenchCAD 2.1 Core, Frontier Code 2.0, Harvey-LAB-AA v1.1, DGPval v2 (internal) and Automation Bench 1.0.6.</p>
<p>In most of those graphs, the maximum scores of Fable and Opus are similar to those of Sol and Astra, but Sol and Astra get to any given level at a dramatically lower price.</p>
<p>They are as always somewhat selected so that they will look as much as possible like this, <a href="https://openai.com/index/introducing-gpt-6-2-astra-sol/">you can click through to their post if you want to see the rest</a>, this one is listed first and is the most comical and extreme, hey look how much Fable 5.2 costs when you set it to max for no reason (I assume):</p>

<h4>Other People’s Benchmarks</h4>


<p>I realize all the reasons why you need to take price into account, but I do still find charts like this useful so here you go:</p>

<figure>
<div>

</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Artificial Analysis still has Fable 5.2 in the lead if you set it to max. If you set Fable 5.2 to a more reasonable xhigh then everyone is tied at 62. Astra is on the cost-score frontier.</p>
