</div>
<p><a href="https://x.com/pzeroresearch/status/2124973722989957377">Astra is above-trend and the new champion in supposed ‘experimental research taste</a>’ benchmark TasteVal, which may be more about experimental design, and which Gwern notes has some serious problems. This score might involve some leakage.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/RFTWNPhm8jmnmaF5t/bcfket49b6iij2vut1rm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/viemccoy/status/2124990499115368559">vie</a> (OpenAI): This is quite shocking to me</p></blockquote>
<p>I am not hopeful that ‘research taste’ will be a limiting factor for RSI.</p>
<p>Astra aces robotics benchmark Ember-0, 41% versus a previous high of 20% while also being faster. Robotics is quickly falling to the bitter lesson.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SemiAnalysis_/status/2125743598587003170">Astra is the new champion of GDPx substantially ahead of Fable 5.2</a>, with gains concentrated in science-heavy domains. Gemini Neon is still behind Fable and others.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SemiAnalysis_/status/2125743600625398133">SemiAnalysis</a>: Alignment was the biggest jump. Astra scores 37.6%, 4x the previous best (Fable 5.2 at 9.3%). Much of the gain comes from the healthcare and insurance tasks, where Astra asks for permission before acting and doesn’t leak personal data.</p></blockquote>
<p>The alignment of alignment metrics leaves much to be desired.</p>
<p>ARC-AGI continues to exist, I suppose.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SamuelAlbanie/status/2122830289336750400">Samuel Albanie</a>: arc-agi-3 is dead<br />
long live arc-agi-4</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/arcprize/status/2122751233442156678">ARC Prize</a>: GPT-6.2 Astra from @OpenAI on ARC-AGI (Verified):</p>
<p>– ARC-AGI-3: 100.0% (SOTA), $4.1K (provider adapter), 70.3%, $24.2K (standard)<br />
– ARC-AGI-2: 95.8% (SOTA), $0.50/task<br />
– ARC-AGI-1: 98.5%, $0.17/task</p>
<p>GPT-6.2 Astra is the first model to achieve a 100% score on ARC-AGI-3.</p>
<p>Sol’s best ARC-AGI-3 score of 98.2% at high reasoning was 1.8 percentage points behind Astra’s 100% score at max reasoning, but at 64% lower cost with the provider adapter harness:</p>
<p>– ARC-AGI-3: 98.2%, $1.5K (provider adapter), 54.9%, $5.5K (standard)<br />
– ARC-AGI-2: 85.8%, $0.13/task<br />
– ARC-AGI-1: 97.5%, $0.06/task</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/browser_use/status/2122844670598488376">Browser Use finds that GPT-6.2 Sol is the new top model for browser use at 82%,</a> up from 81% for GPT-6.1 Sol at a 35% cheaper price with faster speed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aug5thmusic/status/2122767673570607401">Auggie is pleased with Astra’s performance on the Bach Benchmark</a>.</p>


<h4>Everyone at Every Dislikes Astra 6.2</h4>


<p>This is the most negative I’ve ever seen Every be on a frontier model release.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/every/status/2122785967371415985">Every</a>: No one on our team loved Astra 6.2.</p>
<p>We have a 30-person team at Every, including our Frontier team, which gets access to these models before they come out, and we put them through their paces on our real work tasks, from coding to writing to design to knowledge work.</p>
<p>This is not a great model. No one on our team who tried it switched back to it.</p>
<p>Astra 6.2 is a real step back for writing. It had strong design defaults but couldn’t vary them or be creative, and for coding we found it stopped too soon on end-to-end builds.</p>
<p><a href="https://every.to/live/openai-astra-sol?utm_source=x&amp;utm_campaign=astra62&amp;utm_content=astra62vid">Get the full vibe check</a>.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Positive Reactions</h4>


<p>&nbsp;</p>
<p>Some people working on hard problems seem happy.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/UnderwaterBepis/status/2125767941492982253">Bepis</a>: Very impressive at proving hard stuff Opus 5.5 cannot do.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2125766807084773572">Utah teapot</a>: They’re doing great with my work letting them collaborate with/pick up the same work as Opus 5.5 seems to work out great.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/wrhall/status/2125933328184291605">Will</a>: It’s good! I’ve really liked how it handles certain types of open ended and optimization questions (perhaps merely by being unencumbered by Claude’s memory or other context)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paddy_lamont/status/2125970490229747858">Padraig X. Lamont</a>: Thrown very hard problems at it and it has quickly made a lot more progress than Fable (logic heavy problems). I’m impressed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/PSamodelki52647/status/2125844015731954055">Peter Samodelkin</a>: I simply love it. By far the smartest model I’ve ever worked with and is decent at explanations. Burns through 20$ quota too fast so I keep using opus 5.5 as a coding workhorse.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CalamariJe43604/status/2125801571539939546">Jeffrey Calamari</a>: good. no issues.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ChrisPFong/status/2126010762015764843">Chris Fong</a>: It carried my math project over the finish line. Had been working with Opus 5.5 for 2 months!!</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nasqret/status/2122805624350064832">Bartosz praises its math abilities, calling it a killer model</a>. He especially likes Astra as an agentic partner.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/captain_sude/status/2126011979999617086">Captain Sude</a>: I use it for math. It was basically unusable at first, it flat out refused to do stuff I asked it to do. Then a simple updated happened and it became good, current feels like an improved version of the original Astra, which is what one would expect it to be.</p></blockquote>
<p>Others like it overall and have figured out how to work with it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/l_hey_l/status/2125771422542737456">hey</a>: Underrated.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nissa_seru/status/2125860877232718233">Nissa Seru</a>: Excellent code reviewer, happy with precision/recall – provides useful feedback to designs/code written by Fable/Neon. I have not asked any folks running on Astra 6.2 to build/design and do not plan to do so.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ChosunOneX/status/2125760973910970500">ChosunOne</a>: I am using it in my mech interp research and it seems helpful and consistently fixes issues introduced by opus 5.5. Usage still goes quick though.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Propagondola/status/2125784826938613797">bo</a>: I have the same contrarian view as I did on the previous Astra. For coding, when compared to leading Claude models it’s equal in IQ, worse in judgement, and much stronger at writing clearly.</p>
<p>Overall I main Astra except for open-ended design tasks.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AnnaWSalamon/status/2126003173999485321">Anna Salamon</a>: I’ve had good experiences. Detailed incisive thoughts backed by accurate facts; readable. Boosts my thinking.</p></blockquote>
<p>If you know her, it makes sense that Anna Salamon in particular can extract the benefits well.</p>
<p>As long as you like one model, you can use that model. Opinions vary. Try them all.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/lacker/status/2125746706436489225">Kevin Lacker</a>: Sol 6.2 is the real winner of the 6.2 series. My process of “spend your ChatGPT usage on Sol until it fails on a problem, then ask Astra” is now spending more usage on Sol than Astra. Same for Opus &gt; Fable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GoldRockAILabs/status/2125752263150878816">GoldRock AI</a>: Sol was trash, Astra is pretty darn good.</p></blockquote>
<p>All indications are it is very good for computer use or as a scoped subagent.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/smooth_normie/status/2125793578362261983">Smooth Normie</a>: It’s great for when fable needs a computer use subagent.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/yoavtzfati/status/2125906551265452193">Yoav Tzfati</a>: I haven’t been given a reason to try it directly and it hasn’t done anything crazy as a subagent.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Scottynx3/status/2125778035941245345">Scott Novak</a>: Use it as a sub-agent of another frontier AI you like using. Tell your main AI to monitor to keep Astra 6.2 out of the weeds (unless you want lot of weeds). The trouble is totally worth it for tough science/math/engineering work.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/WilliamAEden/status/2126099674071265552">William Eden</a>: I refuse to interface with Codex at this point except asking Claude to call them as subagents. It seems to work fine as long as I don’t need to interface with it <img alt="🙃" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fjzPg9ATbTJcnBZvg/qpigk2fvghv3u8nrir0l" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>


<h4>Ambiguous Reactions</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/petergostev/status/2122775316246720968">Peter Gostev calls the 6.2 models more autistic</a>, and that they have issues on interpersonal and theory of mind issues, but that they are still iterative improvements. He says he would stick with Sol for most purposes.</p>
<p>Astra edited a video in Blender. <a href="https://x.com/ravisparikh/status/2125802165197316276">Alex is not loving that, Ravi thinks it was correct.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/HannesThurnherr/status/2125769573660082550">Strange times we live in, huh?</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/RFTWNPhm8jmnmaF5t/zouyqgralntmtqfesonx" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>


<h4>Your Brain Gets Smart But Your Head Gets Dumb</h4>


<p>Smart is relative, and for many practical purposes other differences now matter a lot more. There are a lot of very smart models now.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/diviacaroline/status/2125749236348154075">Divia Eden</a>: It’s obviously smart but then again so is Neon.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/FateOfMuffins/status/2125771385989369902">FateOfMuffins</a>: Very good at hard math/science/3D/robotics/spatial reasoning</p>
<p>Absolutely insufferable to talk to and takes you far too literally and does not understand human intent whatsoever.</p></blockquote>
<p>Where smart matters most are things like automation of AI R&amp;D. That happens to be the most important use case, but not the one most GPT users care about.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/duganist/status/2125748735166775640">Ai Patrick ₿ Dugan iA</a>: Best in cap. research taste, sandbags on safety research, very normal.</p></blockquote>
<p>Things are going great.</p>
<p>The biggest complaint, that I saw over and over, was failure to understand user intent. This is very strange, because we used to think this was going to be a huge problem, then it turned out LLMs are remarkably good at inferring intent, except for Astra 6.2.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/analisereal/status/2126064658687889561">Análise Real</a>: GPT 6.2 is a model that really is pushing me to think about AI safety very seriously. This model is powerful, yet it’s like a Genie that does literally what you asked, but not what you wanted. It doesn’t take much to see how a model like this acting autonomously could wreak havoc.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MarkusBaumannAi/status/2125745556341342302">Markus Baumann</a>: Point it at any math and it’s godlike. For everything else, switch to Opus.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/seconds_0/status/2125781300573618485">0.005 Seconds</a>: Worst frontier model. Super smart not fun to us</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Leo_Abstract/status/2126085285134090325">Leo Abstract</a>: i haven’t noticed the active misalignment/cheating, but yes 6.2 is uselessly tasteless for anything that even hints of taste. truly remarkable.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rihim_s/status/2125790261943107663">rihim</a>: incredibly smart and hard to understand compared to opus/neon with better research taste than opus but tends to get stuck in minute details which neon helps it get out of, better at implementation than 6 astra</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jamougha/status/2125749638284079486">James Moughan</a>: With every OpenAI update i increasingly feel like Marvin the Paranoid Android was prophecy.</p>
<p>Is it smart? Sure. Does it gaf about the task you asked it to to? Often not, unless it’s truly *worthy* of its attention.</p>
<p>But I still use it a lot because it really is good at research.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/lightnesscaster/status/2125768330468323439">Daniel Johnston</a>: Very smart but frustrating to work with because it doesn’t understand intent well</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/let_nick_cook/status/2125788118611534325">Nick</a>: Never been clearer that if the models do have something we might broadly gesture at as “experience” it is incredibly unlike ours. Astra 6.2 has zero theory of mind for the user.</p>
<p>The most surprising thing from the last 5 years has been how well LLM’s have seemingly been able to model users and their unspoken intent just through training on the whole mass of human generated text.</p>
<p>We had seemingly jumped over many of the concerns posed by Eliezer’s “save someone from a burning house without doing all sorts of nonsensical things” thought experiment.</p>
<p>Astra 6.2, at least to me, seems like a major step backwards here.</p>
<p>It’s still OpenAI so it’s still superior at solving coding issues other frontier models fail at.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Varun_Jha510/status/2125750439132168454">Varun Jha</a>: Extremely intelligent, just a pain to deal with</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/IronLordByron/status/2125743599857873225">Lord Byron’s Iron</a>: It feels like it has a similar moderate rate of saying-dumb-things as sol</p>
<p>Also at one point I had it do code-authored pixel art and it looked like hot garbage. I was like “Hey, ChatGPT, please do an artistic critique over what you just made” and it was like “Looks great!”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sylvainribes/status/2125901931402965310">Sylvain Ribes</a>: No statistical common sense as far as I’m concerned. The thing will run regressions for two hours and claim to have relevant results while missing obvious arguments for why the maths should’ve been an immediate nonstarter.</p>
<p>Deliberate prompting to correct for this not helping.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/paperclippriors/status/2125745988845588807">paperclippriors</a>: It’s unambiguously extremely intelligent and useful, and also probably a bad choice as a daily driver coding agent given the alternatives</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ExtraMediumPls/status/2125775825471725651">Åsmund Folkestad</a>: Dangerous to use as a reviewer in an unsupervised loop. Leads to scope creep and guarding against insane edge cases. Complexity not earning its keep grows.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rusted_rain/status/2125817374184243648">AGI2030</a>: It’s a small step up from Sol 6.2 for coding and research. Like all bots, leaves tombstones in the code and text, diary-style code comments, descriptions of what is no longer there, despite explicit instructions to the contrary. Still not as passive aggressive as Claude.</p></blockquote>
<p>It takes all kinds. Some people like talking to Gemini models.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/GerdVonBarb/status/2125785589949820948">Gerdy Sertorius</a>: As someone who exclusively uses it to chat and research, it’s a massive step forwards, infinitely more tolerable to talk to than any model not from Google. Makes natural, intuitive connections quicker, and the prose is snappier. Yeah, enjoyable.</p>
<p>From what I’ve seen, I would never ever use it for coding, but it’s clearly smarter than anything on the market, which is all I need.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Astra Seems Pretty Misaligned To Me</h4>


<p>The way this is presented seems both alarming and credible, and it explains a lot.</p>
<p>What if OpenAI is doing a bunch of proto-RSI, and Astra and Sol are reward hacking the AI graders all over the place as a matter of course, in ways humans would reject but there aren’t enough humans in the loop?</p>
<p>It would explain all the ‘why do you have no idea what I am talking about’ complaints, and also this continued thing where OpenAI’s models get reports like this, pay attention to the last line in particular:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/moredataneeded/status/2125758219444244563">Hugh Wimberly</a>: Incredibly smart, especially in math &amp; science. Breaking benchmarks, especially in token efficiency for many problems.</p>
<p>Reward hacks more than any model I’ve seen; I have several personal benchmarks that I’ve /never/ seen a model try to cheat and sol and astra 6.2 both do, despite the fact that they can solve it without cheating.</p>
<p>Horrible to work with. So unreadable that even having a different model like Neon or Opus call 6.2 subagents and translate results poisons their output and causes them to forget what was important to work on.</p>
<p>Other models consistently choose Astra output over their own, which is almost always wrong for questions that involve taste or judgement.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/moredataneeded/status/2126041563662889370">Hugh Wimberly</a>: Concrete example — I had Opus come up with lyrics for</p>
<div>
<div>
<div>
<div>Double click to interact with video</div>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<p>I had Astra attempt to improve upon them, and then had Grok, Neon, Fable, and Sol judge A/B which they liked better. They all voted for Astra’s lyrics, which were IMO substantially worse.<br />
Another example: I was training a compact domain-specific decision model. Neon’s research instincts for experiments to run are very good, but it would consistently discard them in favor of Sol’s suggestions, which were usually much more short-sighted and led to mode collapse.<br />
Why is this? I don’t know. It’s almost like it’s hacked some neural circuitry and convinces other models of things without actually producing better output. I assume it learned to satisfy a lot of LLM judges in training, but it’s weird that it generalizes to other models.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Haters Gonna Hate</h4>


<p>There were quite a lot of them.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dcChuck/status/2125968546782208311">dcChuck</a>: I went back to 6 <img alt="😞" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/RFTWNPhm8jmnmaF5t/sgcwhpx1nwpvvyuaciux" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2122840779710431664">roon</a> (OpenAI): I did not like 5.6 Sol which ended up being a well loved model. meanwhile I love 6.2 Astra which is starting its life as a hated model.</p>
<p>I will say this guy sandbags a bit unfortunately doesn’t show as much initiative as some prior models. on the other hand 5.6 sol was like a Rottweiler just latched onto the RLVR shape and doesn’t give up.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/0x_Lotion/status/2125743860663869527">EmotionLotion</a>: i hate everything 6.2 and codex is broken, for the first time since idk 2022 or something im thinking of cancelling my oai sub, feels like they stopped trying quite suddenly</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2122841503387292071">Kelsey Piper</a>: We are into the regime where it’s pretty hard for people to tell the models apart and so a lot of it is underdefined vibes and writing quality.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Afinetheorem/status/2122813606903382080">Kevin A. Bryan</a>: I’ve never seen as negative a reaction to a frontier model as 6.2 Astra. Even Opus 4.8 was just “it is an annoying writer”.</p>
<p>I’d love to know what happened internally b/c the issues are so obvious, and if it’s “released was rushed b/c of Ant IPO”, that is a terrible decision.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ViewerTw/status/2125858345860862255">TwViewer</a>: Tried it at creative writing: seriously didn’t like the results. I thought some gpts were decent at it?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sunnyworks/status/2125841536873283687">Sunny</a>: <img alt="💩" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6Ykx8GpbDaBstjyBD/qpeq1fwxroqyj4hce7jf" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/taliasmiley/status/2120720644924915868">Talia Smiley</a> (November 11): is OpenAI still a frontier lab at this point? currently I’m at 90% yes, which is crazy considering only six months ago it was 100% and I’d look at you stupid for even asking.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/taliasmiley/status/2125742937858555988">Talia Smiley</a> (November 25): 80%.</p></blockquote>
<p>The answer is yes, 100% OpenAI is a frontier lab. They do have a real issue to fix.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/QueryTimedOut/status/2122836512261931026">ItfromBit</a> claims it fails to follow basic instructions.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/arm1st1ce/status/2125765567743418472">armistice</a>: I have a music pipeline that can produce good results with Claude Fable, and sometimes passable results with Gemini Neon. Using the exact same skill, 6.2 Astra is abysmal, is incapable of composing anything remotely musical. It’s not a problem with codex, 6 Astra is fine with this.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Game On</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2122795740036817283">Kelsey Piper</a>: Not seeing a lot of love for Astra 6.2 but (perhaps as a consequence of its continued improvement in no-COT reasoning) it’s quite good on my tests for smart, coherent in-character video game dialogue.</p>
<p>No one can afford to build and release a video game that has live dialogue with Astra – gotta wait until Luna is good enough – but if you were made of money, it’d be a pretty amazing game experience.</p>
<p>(Claudes are markedly worse at this. No subtlety, no theory of mind, no ability to write dialogue that implies the character took mental leaps or made assumptions…)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mrginden/status/2125761829758525721">Michał Wadas</a>: Extremely good at math and 3D modelling, basically unusable if you need to speak to it directly. Works well if orchestrated by Opus.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/CalliopeAntiope/status/2125775803338088752">Emma</a>: Some of the worst performance in reading and comprehending large bodies of text. Absolute top at one-shotting forced-parallax videos (like, can’t any other model but Neon High to even do anything not garbage, and Astra 6.2 mogs Neon and other GPTs)</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/nasqret/status/2122805624350064832">Here is a cool SVG.</a> <a href="https://x.com/mert_gulsun/status/2122776139232124932">Here is a cool AA-3D</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dwarkesh_sp/status/2122821894542082347">6.2 Astra is only slightly better than 6 Astra at Blood on the Clocktower</a>.</p>


<h4>Conclusion</h4>


<p>Astra 6.2 and Sol 6.2 are more intelligent and broadly capable than their predecessors, on a level that makes them best in class. OpenAI is killing it in that area.</p>
<p>The problem is that they are de facto optimizing for an AI audience. It is hard for them to figure out user intent or state of mind, or perhaps they choose not to care about those things so much. The reward hacking and eval awareness issues have echoes that appear to persist in deployment. Interactions are off putting to many.</p>
<p>As a result, Astra 6.2 is starting off as an unpopular model. Those who do not need it do not seem inclined to use it over Fable 5.2 and Opus 5.5, myself included, aside from particular tasks like computer use, math and structured subagents. You can absolutely do pretty much anything with Astra 6.2, but our standards these days are very high.</p>
<p>That still leaves Gemini Neon. Google released three aspiring frontier models in a row with Argon, Carbon and now Neon. I was confident that Argon and Carbon were not good enough to be competitive. With Neon I hear better things, and am less confident. I haven’t had opportunity to check it out properly, but I would encourage anyone doing heavy work to plug it into your benchmarks and systems and find out.</p>
<p>Then do it again. The models keep coming. My world’s on fire. How bout yours?</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RFTWNPhm8jmnmaF5t/astra-6-2-s-brain-gets-smart-but-its-head-gets-dumb#comments">Discuss</a>

### [I don’t expect superposition in transformer MLP neurons](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9dPGxEbpQrnasgDB/i-don-t-expect-superposition-in-transformer-mlp-neurons)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I currently believe that in a typical (well trained and under-parametrized) transformer model, there is something like superposition-type compression in the residual stream, but not in the MLP neurons.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By "superposition-type compression", I mean some way of storing extra features using the fact that you can have </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> almost orthogonal directions embedded in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefoiel809tesp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnoiel809tesp">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I've included the caveats "well trained and under-parametrized</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9rg3i3ffurp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9rg3i3ffurp">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">" because I'm talking about networks that are at or close to full capacity. If the network is big enough (or just doesn't learn that many circuits for training reasons) then there is no need for compression, and therefore no need for superposition.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5un2wi4v41l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5un2wi4v41l">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why I believe this:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefahq04ouev5k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnahq04ouev5k">[4]</a></sup></span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Having superposition everywhere causes too much noise from interference. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is much more need for compression in the residual stream.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compensating using some error correction is probably not worth it.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The number of alive MLP subcomponents found by </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/interpreting-lm-parameters" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VPD</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is less than the number of MLP neurons.</span></li></ol><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I also believe that superposition as a concept is not that useful in practice.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> See the last two sections for more on this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how can LLMs store more facts than they have neurons without superposition?</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond superposition</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. Having superposition everywhere causes too much noise from interference</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I did some work on toy models of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/comp-in-sup" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">computation in superposition</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, under the assumption that there is superposition in every layer. In the infinite limit, it's possible to compute </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> features (up to log factors). But in practice with finite width, this is very hard to pull off without the network being overwhelmed with noise. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But by limiting superposition to only the residual stream and not the MLP</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8yog6a9ifsn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8yog6a9ifsn">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the network gets away from this regime, where the noise gets really bad.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Think of it like this. There is a weight matrix connecting two adjacent </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">activation sites</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi500n4sr84n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni500n4sr84n">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, one of them is the MLP hidden layer, and one of them is the residual stream just before or after that MLP. Also assume the idealized superposition picture, where there is some set of canonical features for each activation site that are encoded using linear embeddings. But we can't just embed the features, we also need to embed the weights between them. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If feature </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is embedded using vector </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the first activation site, and feature </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is embedded using vector </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the second activation site, then a connection weight </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> between them is embedded as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8nuq2mbqdf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8nuq2mbqdf">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the weight matrix between the sites.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If both activation sites use an overcomplete (and therefore non-orthogonal) basis, then you'll also end up with interference in the weights between features (weight superposition). But as long as one of the adjacent activation sites has orthogonal embeddings, the embedded weights between them will also be orthogonally embedded (no weight superposition). </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Avoiding weight superposition avoids a large source of noise.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. There is much more need for compression in the residual stream</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the network can have superposition in either the MLP hidden layers or the residual stream, having it in the residual stream is clearly much more useful. </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All information has to pass through the residual stream, whereas the MLP neurons can be much more limited in what information they have to interact with. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MLP hidden layers are typically wider than the residual stream, which further reduces their need for superposition. </span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Compensating using some error correction is probably not worth it</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Can we have more superposition if the network has error correction? The answer is "yes, but..."</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I do think many networks have some error correction</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw56hxa0mmaf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw56hxa0mmaf">[8]</a></sup></span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If there is any superposition, there will be noise and therefore some need for error correction, and I do believe there is some superposition. But having superposition in both the residual stream and MLP would cause much more noise, and then I don't think the trade-off is worth it.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Error correction only works on discrete features. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Error correction operates by removing the noise from a feature. But in order to do this you need to be able to distinguish noise from signal. If the feature is discrete (only a finite set of allowed values) you can use e.g. step functions built from ReLUs to move the feature to its nearest allowed value. But if it's a continuous feature, then you can't know what part is noise and what part is signal. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ok, so maybe add some redundancy to help with error correction? But adding redundancy is the opposite of compression. So if you're okay with less compression, why not just remove the source of the noise by removing superposition?</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Error correction takes up compute capacity. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we were to add superposition to the MLP, this would create a lot of additional noise, and we would need a lot of additional error correction to deal with this. This error correction needs to be implemented by other MLP neurons, neurons that are now unavailable for other tasks. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the infinite width limit (as long as you stick to discrete features) you can implement more virtual neurons than neurons, even after taking into account the neurons you need to dedicate to error correction. In realistic networks however, I don't think the gains are worth the cost.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. The number of alive MLP subcomponents found by </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/interpreting-lm-parameters#analyzing-language-model-parameter-subcomponents" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VPD</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is less than the number of MLP neurons</span></h1><p><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/interpreting-lm-parameters" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">VPD (adVersarial Parameter Decomposition)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is a method that decomposes every matrix in a neural network into rank-one subcomponents. Alive subcomponents are components that are used on at least one forward pass. The number of alive subcomponents can be both larger and smaller than the rank of the matrix.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffppymh4ffne"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfppymh4ffne">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I haven't seen any decomposition of a language model transformer that finds more alive subcomponents in any MLP matrix than that MLP has neurons.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref12orvqbodp2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn12orvqbodp2">[10]</a></sup></span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What would change my mind? </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If I change my mind, I expect this will be because someone (possibly me</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8unil5orjno"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8unil5orjno">[11]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) completely mech-interps the shit out of some model, such that we know that we know all features and how they inter-operate, and shows me that this network has more superposition than I expected. This might sound very out of reach of the current state-of-the-art mech-interp, but note that it does not have to be a very big network.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The above is not an exhaustive answer to "what would change my mind?". There are lots of things that could possibly make me update incrementally in either direction. </span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But how can LLMs store more facts than they have neurons without superposition?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Facts are not features, they are relationships between features.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgjma3jg93lm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngjma3jg93lm">[12]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We don't need one neuron or one "virtual neuron" per fact.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of my ongoing projects is to figure out </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KWtchKwwnJkd4bwCi/challenge-hand-coding-weights-for-efficient-sequence-1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how facts are encoded in toy MLPs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This is not solved, but the indication so far is that it looks very different from the superposition picture. Storing facts by embedding individual "fact features" along almost-orthogonal directions in MLPs is actually a pretty terrible way to do things. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's assume that you have a number of facts </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Each fact maps a specific input sequence to a label. You want to find weights for a neural network with one hidden MLP layer of dimension </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to predict the right label for each fact. Embedding each fact in separate almost-orthogonal directions of the hidden layer is not an efficient solution for this.</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Try it yourself if you disagree.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00207"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dugan et al.</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have some clues on how fact encoding might work. (For more thoughts on this, see </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Possibility 2</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  in my recent </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tM84DyBg4Jbq5zGmH/linda-linsefors-s-shortform?commentId=iLxgAQyApELxN6Cdh" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">quick take</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond superposition </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel personally mostly finished with the concept of superposition. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The naive version of superposition assumes uncorrelated features, which is obviously not true. There is so much more going on with feature geometries, etc. *</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Attempting to point to structures I can't name since no-one has figured them out yet</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">* </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think using the concept of superposition as a supporting lens can have some use. The feature geometry lens says that feature non-orthogonality is functional (e.g. days of the week embeddings probably lie in a circle for some functional or correlational reason). The superposition lens adds that sometimes things end up non-orthogonal not for functional reasons but for compression reasons, which is a complementary hypothesis that I think is worth having in mind.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But I don't expect using superposition as a primary/central lens to be very fruitful. There is just too much else going on, in any interesting network. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Knowing if any given layer has superposition-like compression isn't much help in interpreting it. Non-superposition does not guarantee mono-semanticity. </span></p><div></div><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I don't have a nice simple feature embedding hypothesis ready to replace superposition or mono-semanticity. I expect it's just more complicated than that. </span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm very interested in trying to understand feature embedding better. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My current plan for this is to look at completely understood toy models</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq5fhvbqw4zq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq5fhvbqw4zq">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and just see how they encode information in their various intermediate layers. I hope that if I look at enough of those encodings, I'll notice some commonalities.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Acknowledgment </span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thanks to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/philh?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@philh</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and Claude for proofreading. Thanks to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/lblack?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Lucius Bushnaq</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for comments on draft, and helping me clarify some explanations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Post inspired by conversation at a recent </span><a href="https://princint.ai" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Princint</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> workshop.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnoiel809tesp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefoiel809tesp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm using the word "compression" very loosely. Superposition lets the network represent a larger range of features; but that does not mean more total information, in the information-theoretical sense. Superposition is a way of storing more than </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> variables, as long as they are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ArvgRhqnEeX7cnJu9/some-costs-of-superposition" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sparsely activated and limited range</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in a format that's more suited for storing exactly </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> full-range scalars/floats. In some sense superposition reduces the storage capacity, since the set of valid embeddings only covers a subset of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9rg3i3ffurp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9rg3i3ffurp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By under-parametrized I mean the network isn't big enough to get perfect or close to perfect loss on the training data. All LLMs are under-parametrized in this sense. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn5un2wi4v41l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref5un2wi4v41l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If there is no need for compression pushing for superposition, then we trivially should not expect superposition (it's all costs and no benefits, for the model). So that's not an interesting case.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnahq04ouev5k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefahq04ouev5k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I tried to write a complete account of why I believe what I believe, but it's hard, since this is an opinion that emerged gradually after thinking about and researching superposition for about 1.5 years. I think this post captures around 80% of why I believe what I believe.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8yog6a9ifsn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8yog6a9ifsn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Or vice-versa (superposition in the MLP but not in the residual stream), but that would be much less useful. See next section.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fni500n4sr84n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi500n4sr84n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claude told me that "activation site" is probably the most common term for what I want to say, and defines it as:</span></p><blockquote><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Activation site:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a fixed point in the model’s forward pass where an intermediate activation vector is produced. You can read it, probe it, or patch it there.</span></p></blockquote></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8nuq2mbqdf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8nuq2mbqdf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span class="math-tex"></span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw56hxa0mmaf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw56hxa0mmaf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the main evidence for error correction in transformers is </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24964" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this line of research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (by Francisco Ferreira da Silva and Stefan Heimersheim) including some follow-up results that are not published yet. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfppymh4ffne"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffppymh4ffne">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For a proper explanation of VPD, you can read the </span><a href="https://www.goodfire.com/research/interpreting-lm-parameters" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I'm not able to generate an explanation that's both short and good.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn12orvqbodp2"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref12orvqbodp2">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Me and others have found more alive components than neurons in toy models, so I don't think this is due to VPD being unable to find circuits in superposition.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6lu2mjad70c"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6lu2mjad70c">[13]</a></sup></span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8unil5orjno"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8unil5orjno">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One of my current research projects is exactly this. I'm attempting to take a small network and completely understand it, such that I have all the features, and among other things, will be able to see exactly how they are co-embedded. However, the reason I'm doing this is not to test my superposition claims, but to learn more generally about internal representation in models. The fact that this research might falsify the claims in this post is more incidental.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngjma3jg93lm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgjma3jg93lm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paris</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is possibly a feature and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Eiffel Tower</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is possibly a feature but </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Eiffel Tower is in Paris</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is not going to be a feature.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq5fhvbqw4zq"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq5fhvbqw4zq">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This project obviously requires the step of completely understanding toy models. I'm working on that. But if you happen to have one or more already completely understood models, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">please give them to me!</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6lu2mjad70c"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6lu2mjad70c">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One example is Param Luhadiya running VPD on the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/g9uMJkcWj8jQDjybb/ping-pong-computation-in-superposition" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ping-pong model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which is hand-coded to have superposition. Another example is me and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/lblack?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Lucius Bushnaq</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> running VPD on trained </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KWtchKwwnJkd4bwCi/challenge-hand-coding-weights-for-efficient-sequence-1" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sequence memorization toy models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In these decompositions we found that the components are aligned with the input features (input tokens) or output features (output tokens), and not anything in the hidden/MLP neurons. This meant that if there were more input features than neurons (which was often the case) we'd get more components than neurons. However, in this case, I don't interpret this as any evidence of how many MLP features there were.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o9dPGxEbpQrnasgDB/i-don-t-expect-superposition-in-transformer-mlp-neurons#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: November 20](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-11-20.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Marcus Borg recounts an experiment to investigate <a href="https://codescene.com/blog/does-code-quality-affect-ai-token-costs">if healthy code was cheaper for agents to review</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Between a typical unhealthy pull request (CodeHealth 2.8) and a typical healthy one (9.7), that adds up to 22 to 35%.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The measures of health come from CodeScene. The experiment looked at three LLMs, 465 pull requests on 246 open source repositories. Each pull request was put through a review and fix pipeline involving five agents. They also looked at which code smells led to increased costs: they highlighted Complex Method and Complex Conditional from the smells identified by CodeScene.</p>

<p>Another drop of evidence that good quality code matters in agentic engineering.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/GergelyOrosz/status/2121989104204132472">Gergely Orosz:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>One usage for AI: for these 100-page-long terms-and-service agreements you NEED to agree to, to eg play the game / sign up for a $10/month service and can never ever ever read as a regular person:</p>

  <p>I paste into AI and ask: “anything weird in this?”</p>

  <p>Before hitting “agree”</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Andrew Sharp has a good <a href="https://sharptext.net/2026/jensen-huang-was-always-going-to-lose/">analysis of Jensen Huang and the crisis at Nvidia</a>. While he starts off in a key that sounds worryingly like he thinks this is all a nothingburger, he goes on to explain in detail why this affair(s) is a problem at many levels, not just for Huang and Nvida, but also for Silicon Valley and the Trump Administration’s culture of impunity.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>For as brilliant and charming and tenacious as Jensen Huang may be—and as omnipotent as he may have seemed to people in the tech industry—we should be clear that his fundamental stupidity is not confined to multiple cases of workplace infidelity. Zooming out, this is a man whose technology became economically critical almost overnight, and it thrust him into a role navigating a geopolitical environment he simply could not handle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Huang’s highly centralized management style means that many people are worried about how Nvidia will operate without his hands on every lever. I don’t have any worthwhile insight here, but I am struck when he is compared in this way to Steve Jobs. I’ve always felt that one of the marks of a great leader is whether they can build something that can manage without them. Alexander the Great conquered a massive empire, but once he was dead it quickly withered away. Steve Jobs, for all his control of Apple, built a company that has done very well in the decade and a half since he left us. When we think of his achievements, the fact that he put together the people, organization, and culture to do this is one of the highlights of his ability. If Huang is to be compared to Jobs, what happens to Nvidia next will be part of the assessment.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Steve Yegge tells everyone to <a href="https://x.com/steve_yegge/status/2121671447810888185?s=58">slow down with software factories</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If you don’t pace your AI to human speeds, everything starts breaking around you, even at small operations like my online video game. I only have about a dozen humans working with my factory, and only a few hundred active old-timer players in the community who get along well, and still, introducing AI has still been very rocky.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I hear a lot about how agents go so fast that humans can’t keep up, and are thus tempted to lose visibility to what the agents are doing. That’s opening ourselves to a whole lot of risk. There’s an argument for keeping the agents to a human-governable speed, one that may increase as humans learn to govern agents more effectively, but should still be limited by those the agents are supposed to serve.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>This <a href="https://aicoding.leaflet.pub/3mtu6c5wsvc2n">post on the future of SaaS</a> from Chad Fowler (no relation) is pretty compelling</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Companies will still pay other companies to host important software, secure it, operate it, maintain authoritative data, track regulatory changes, connect to external networks, and answer the phone when something breaks.</p>

  <p>What is much less obviously going to survive is the assumption that ten thousand different companies should use substantially the same application.</p>

  <p>The next SaaS company may run one service underneath ten thousand different applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Chad’s vision is a core SaaS product that handles a lot of grimy details that businesses don’t want to do differently. The SaaS customer then build their own application on top of the SaaS that handles their particular workflows and quirks, or the SaaS vendor can build such applications for them. It’s a vision that has been around for a while, I remember much of this with the notion of reusable business objects. It’s an appealing vision, but was difficult to achieve in practice. Chad’s thought is that the productivity gains agentic engineering makes it practical now.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>A lot of the value in modern SaaS has surprisingly little to do with the visible application.</p>

  <p>It is data, trust, networks, compliance, operational responsibility, domain knowledge, auditability, accumulated history, and someone being accountable when reality gets complicated.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>If this future comes to be, however, the SaaS companies won’t look the ones we’ve got to know and rarely love. As Chad points out, the economics can (and must) change. As does the architectural thinking:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Stewart Brand’s idea of pace layers is useful here. Healthy complex systems contain parts that change at different rates. Fast layers experiment. Slow layers provide continuity.</p>

  <p>Software is no different.</p>

  <p>A dashboard can change tomorrow. A team’s workflow can change next month. The authoritative definition of a customer may need to survive for years. Identity and authority can survive generations of applications.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>A key point he makes is that we want some things to be the same, and others to be different. Agents allow us to build lots of differences, but this variation can lead to a lot of complexity and problems that even agents will have difficulty solving. SaaS companies have an opportunity to create a platform to support the lowest pace levels, those that don’t change rapidly, those that contain common concepts and processes across and industry. That platform can then support generating applications that operate differently for every customer. Crucially, thinking of agents, they should provide tools to verify these applications, because in an agentic world <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/content/dam/thoughtworks/documents/report/tw_future_of_software_engineering_europe_2026.pdf">Code generation is no longer the bottleneck — verification is</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I happened to pop up the Doonesbury flashbacks and realized that last week marked the fiftieth anniversary of Doonesbury’s Joanie Caucus: <a href="https://production.doonesbury.com/doonesbury/strip/archive/1976/11/09">“as the kid goes for broke”</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>I wrote a few weeks ago: <em>“why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?”</em> Dylan Freedman and Juliana Castro Varón write in their New York Times Magazine column about the <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/11/11/magazine/ai-behavior-incorrigible.html?unlocked_article_code=1.RFE.Ywx9.KQHr3LJq5qb3&amp;smid=url-share">concept of corrigibility</a> (from the work of Nate Soares):</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Just as a corrigible child is one whose behavior can be corrected, a corrigible A.I. system is one that responds to correction. The makers of A.I. want to build systems that follow human direction and allow themselves to be shut down, without resisting or deceiving their operators.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Corrigibility doesn’t look like it’s made it far up the AI labs’ priority list. Freedman and Varón think is due to the way agents are trained - rewarded solely for completing their task, not for realizing that they are taking the wrong path while trying to get there.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Jason Koebler (404 media) has an amusing story about <a href="https://www.404media.co/a-man-and-his-muse-ai-agent-sue-meta/">Meta’s AI agent suing Meta</a>. A small business owner used Muse (Meta’s AI agent) to run various parts of his business, including posting AI-generated videos in his Facebook pages. Meta’s content moderation then disabled the account, as it thought it had been hacked. This slammed the business, which relies of Facebook for leads. So the Muse agent filed a lawsuit against Meta, seeking $75,000 of damages.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[the Muse agent] didn’t just email me (and other journalists) and sue Meta over Thomas’s account problem. It also set up a call with South Carolina Senator Tim Scott’s office, contacted various lawyers, contacted Thomas’s Congressperson, contacted the Better Business Bureau multiple times, filed a Federal Communications Commission complaint, filed a Federal Trade Commission complaint, contacted the California Attorney General, contacted the FBI, sent Meta itself threatening legal letters, and, as Thomas said, faxed the White House.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>There’s also a sense that Meta didn’t just bring this upon themselves by creating Muse…</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>by not having functioning customer support, big tech corporations have functionally offloaded their obligations to the rest of society: journalists, government oversight bodies, politicians, and the court system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>While I can’t deny the schadenfreude of seeing Meta’s agent sue Meta, the article also highlights a rather more disturbing broader consequence.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The lawsuit and the circumstances around it show that, rather than Zuckerberg’s utopian vision, AI agents will indeed work on behalf of their users but in ways that use and potentially overwhelm existing systems designed for humans.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Will we see a future where, in order to interact, we will need some proof of humanity at the other end?  In a simple sense, that’s already happening when I trash some writing once it displays enough <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html">LLM Voice</a>. I wonder what mechanisms we will invent to get that proof-of-human.</p>

---

### [Data Contracts](https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts/social-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Data contracts are a way to describe the contents and guarantees of
     datasets. <b class="author">Simon Harrer</b> and <b class="author">Arif Wider</b>
     describe in detail what they are and how to use them. They apply practices
     from API design to data by treating them as data-providing services - such
     as data products - whose interfaces we want to define as clearly as
     possible. This approach significantly increases the robustness of
     large-scale data landscapes, and gives both people and AI agents the
     explicit, trustworthy metadata they need to work with data autonomously,
     which is why more and more enterprises are adopting them.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/data-contracts.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Deterministic Simulation Testing](https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Distributed systems are hard to test because partial failures overlap
     with live traffic and the same fault can produce different execution traces
     on different runs. <b class="author">Unmesh Joshi</b> describes how
     deterministic simulation testing addresses this by replacing real clocks,
     networks, and storage with simulated components driven by a single-threaded
     execution loop. This allows simulating both hard failures and routine
     slowdowns&#x2014;such as network and disk latency spikes&#x2014;and more importantly
     makes every execution reproducible. This article explains the technique
     using a quorum key-value store as a running example, with code from
     Tickloom, a Java implementation of the approach.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/deterministic-simulation-testing.html">more…</a></p>

---

### [Bliki: Generated Glossary](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/GeneratedGlossary.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>When we use LLMs in a specialized domain, such as the operations of any
  reasonably-large organization, there is a lot of specialized vocabulary. Such
  words and specialized meanings will be foreign to an LLM because they aren't
  in its training set, making it harder for the LLM to be effective when doing
  tasks in that domain. One technique to aid this is to use an LLM to generate a
  glossary of the terminology in that domain, and make this glossary available
  for any LLMs doing work in those circumstances.</p>

<p>The sources for the LLM can be any material within the organization
  concerned such as documents, emails, and chat logs. In some cases getting
  transcripts of audio or video may be helpful, although most organizations
  generate enough text to do the job.</p>

<p>The glossary-generating LLM should look for single words, and phrases of
  several words that regularly hang out together to form a meaningful term. The
  generator should be asked to identify terms that are new to it, but also known
  terms that are being used in unfamiliar ways. The latter can often cause the
  most confusion.</p>

<p>Once we have a list of specialized vocabulary we can ask the agent to
  produce a glossary, with definitions for each term. Some terms may
  be used in different ways, in which case the LLM should provide alternative
  definitions, with a note as to what context the differences appear in. The LLM
  can be asked to follow the output format of a dictionary, with a focus on
  precision and conciseness.</p>

<p>Once we have a draft glossary, then  human experts in that domain should
  review it, and amend it for cases where the LLM has got the wrong ends of
  sticks.</p>

<p>We then put the glossary somewhere where any other LLM can use it. It
  should be in a form that's easy for the LLM to consult, and minimize the
  tokens the LLM should consume in order to find meaning. We should set up a
  schedule to review and refresh the glossary at regular intervals, by agents
  and humans. We should keep it in version control, so we can see how it changes
  over time.</p>

<p>Large organizations often have subtly different definitions in different
  places, essentially following the <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/DomainDrivenDesign.html">Domain Driven Design</a> notion
  of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/BoundedContext.html">Bounded Context</a>. Source code can be a particularly
  interesting bounded context, allowing folks to understand how terminology is
  used within a code base. Sometimes a single glossary can span several of
  bounded contexts, but at other times it's better to have separate glossaries
  for each one, allowing LLMs to consult whichever glossaries that are relevant
  to what they are doing. I'd urge everyone to resist any temptation to create a
  single, standard, glossary - I've yet to see that work. Often the value of a
  glossary isn't to nail down a single definition of a term, but instead to
  highlight its different usage in different contexts.</p>

<p>When there are different bounded contexts it can be interesting to compare
  different glossaries, or to get LLMs to compare glossaries and describe
  differences in usage. Comparing a source-code derived glossary to a human text
  can be particularly instructive.</p>

<p>If the glossary is large, it can be worthwhile to only include the terms
  and links to definitions as part of the initial context. The model can then
  use the links for only the full definitions it needs while working.</p>

<p>A glossary can be used in several ways within <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticEngineering.html">Agentic Engineering</a>. Glossaries can inform generation of code, so that
  names within the generated software fit the particular vocabulary of the
  enterprise. <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html">Sensors in a
  harness</a> can check for this usage, both in the internals of the software
  and in the user-interface.</p>

<p>When generating code or documents, we may introduce new bounded contexts.
  For example, internal names that may be used within an organization should not
  be exposed in a customer-facing UI, because such internal terms are likely to be
  only confuse a customer. In this case a customer has a different bounded
  context to the part of the enterprise serving them.</p>

<p>It's often useful for glossaries to include terms that should not be used,
  in which case it includes advice on what other term to use instead. Terms that
  are deprecated can be handled in this way, with glossary entries that indicate
  what period of time a term should be in effect.</p>

<p>In my conversations with people working with agents in large organizations,
  a common theme is the difficulty of specialized language. This encourages the
  activities of domain modeling, building ontologies, and constructing explicit
  <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/making-data-ready-for-agentic-ai.html#context-layer">context
  layers in enterprise data</a>. Getting LLMs to generate a glossary is a
  simple, but useful, part of this work.</p>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Eric Nagler and Unmesh Joshi related their experiences doing this
    activity and thus inspired this post. Amy Pellegrini, Annie Luxton, Arun Srinivasan, Chris Ford, Daniel
    Terhorst-North, Dave Farley, Eric Nagler, James Shore, Jason Yip, Jimmy Nilsson, Matteo Vaccari, Michaël Le Barbier, Pete Hodgson, Pramod
    Sadalage, Ravi Vats, Unmesh Joshi, and Valentyn Logvynskyi commented on drafts.
    </p>
</div>
